#269. How Beta Technologies Is Clearing the Runway for Electric Flight

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It aims to lift the industry with its aircraft, motors, batteries, and charging network

Beta Technologies, one of the top three U.S. electric aircraft makers, is aiming first at regional cargo and medical transport, before pursuing the passenger market. Shown here is Beta’s Alia A250, for vertical takeoffs and landings.

In the cockpit of Beta Technologies’ Alia CX300, pilot Chris Caputo steers onto Runway 33 at Patrick Leahy Burlington International Airport in Vermont. Cleared for takeoff, he releases the brakes and nudges the throttle forward.

At the back of the aircraft, the five-blade pusher propeller snaps awake, and the Alia bolts forward. There’s no shriek of a turbofan spooling up, no shudder of a piston engine, and no wait for thrust. Just the low hum of an electric motor and a shove of instant acceleration.

Within a few seconds, we’re at 85 knots (almost 160 kilometers per hour), and Caputo rotates the nose upward. The runway drops abruptly away as we climb. Gaining speed and altitude, we head northwest over Lake Champlain. Through the wraparound canopy, I watch the hunched shoulders of Vermont’s Green Mountains shrink under the right wing, with New York’s taller Adirondacks to the left.

Caputo, a former Air Force fighter pilot, flicks the control stick hard left, then right. Despite its 15-meter wingspan, the Alia feels snappy, its fly-by-wire system automatically coordinating aileron and rudder inputs. The machine, he says, is much simpler to manage than the Cessna 182 I flew around Australia in my student-pilot days.

What’s more, our half-hour flight in the Alia will consume only about US $9 worth of electricity. The same flight in a comparable Cessna 208 Caravan would burn roughly 25 to 30 gallons of Jet A fuel, costing about $210 to $255 at press time. “Even at full throttle, we’re not burning any fuel up here,” says Caputo, as he takes the Alia into a sweeping left turn and heads south over the lake. “We’re not polluting the environment. [There’s] no noise pollution.”

At Beta’s South Burlington, Vt., facility, workers assemble the body of an Alia A250. The company says it has an order and option-to-buy backlog of nearly 1,000 aircraft.Beta Technologies

This ride is happening during an unusually lively time for electric aviation. The promise of lower operating costs and cleaner flight has pushed electric aircraft from a fringe experiment to one of the aviation industry’s most closely watched frontiers. After years of renderings, prototypes, and overly optimistic timetables, companies are now flying full-size aircraft, submitting hardware to regulators for certification, building factories, and testing routes with prospective customers.

Among the top three U.S. developers of electric planes, Archer Aviation and Joby Aviation are concentrating on piloted vertical-takeoff-and-landing air taxis, otherwise known as eVTOLs. Beta Technologies is taking a broader—and in some ways more innovative—path into the market. Rather than betting first on air taxis, Beta is looking to enter electric aviation through short-haul freight planes that use conventional runways, fed by an open charging network that’s designed to make electric aircraft—both Beta’s and its competitors’—part of a working transportation system.

To that end, the company is developing two variants of its Alia airframe: the runway-based CX300 I’m flying in and the A250, which adds four lift propellers for vertical takeoff and landing. Beta says its test pilots have flown the Alia family more than 300,000 kilometers, including doing multiple U.S. crossings and overseas demonstrations. The company claims an order and option-to-buy backlog of 991 aircraft.

RELATED: From Hockey Enforcer to High-Flying eVTOL CEO

“We’re taking a pragmatic stepwise approach for both certification and market entry,” says Beta’s CEO and founder Kyle Clark. “We’re ready to go today with medical and cargo aircraft that fly from airport to airport.” Next up, he says, will be passenger aircraft with conventional takeoff and landing, followed by cargo eVTOLs, and finally passenger eVTOLs.

Beta’s 17,500-square-meter production facility sits beside the Burlington airport and looks more like a giant sunlit workshop than a conventional airplane factory. Geothermal heat pumps help heat and cool the structure. The roof will eventually hold a megawatt of solar panels. On the floor, technicians make composite structures and join aircraft sections beneath American and Pride flags.

3 Competing Paths to Electric AviationThe top three U.S. electric-aircraft makers are taking different approaches to the same challenge: how to make short-hop flight cleaner, quieter, and commercially useful.aspect_ratioJoby Aviation’s piloted, four-passenger S4 aircraft has six tilting propellers to provide both vertical lift and cruise propulsion. It’s optimized for fast, quiet air-taxi service. The company is also working on hydrogen fuel cells for longer-range transport. Joby says the S4 is in the final stage of the Federal Aviation Administration’s certification process and has flown trials in Dubai, New York, and Texas.Archer Aviation’s piloted, four-passenger Midnight has 12 propellers—six tilting rotors and six stationary rotors. The aircraft, which Archer has been testing in both vertical and conventional takeoffs and landings, is aimed at air-taxi service in urban areas. The company says Midnight is in Phase 4 of the FAA certification process and has completed trial flights.Beta Technologies’ piloted Alia aircraft comes in two flavors: The A250 eVTOL has separate vertical-lift rotors and a rear cruise propeller, while the CX300 is a conventional-takeoff fixed-wing plane. The two models use the same pusher motor, battery pack, flight control computer, and fixed-pitch propeller. The company is initially focusing on cargo and medical transport, regional logistics, and airport-to-airport operations, with passenger service later. Beta expects FAA certification for the CX300 in 2027 and for the A250 a year later.Kyle Clark, a native of Vermont, founded Beta in 2017 to realize his long-held dream of building a hybrid-electric aircraft. (For his bachelor’s thesis at Harvard University, he designed a flight-control system for such an aircraft.) Martine Rothblatt, CEO of the biotech company United Therapeutics and cofounder of SiriusXM satellite radio, invested $48 million in Beta to fund its first prototype, with the eventual goal of using the aircraft to transport organs for transplantation.The company has kept much of its design and manufacturing in-house, partly because the pieces are closely coupled and partly because suitable aviation components didn’t exist when development began. Vertical integration gives Beta’s engineers control over the interfaces and any failure data. But it also means the company must be competent at several businesses simultaneously: airframes, electric motors, inverters, batteries, software, chargers, and aircraft production.The production line can build both the fixed-wing CX300 and the vertical-lift A250, which share the same fuselage, wings, pusher motor (for horizontal propulsion), batteries, avionics, and much of the tooling. This results in some one-size-fits-all decisions: The CX300’s twin booms, which connect the wing to the tail surfaces, look superfluous until you see the A250’s four lift propellers mounted on similar structures.On the CX300, those booms add weight and drag even though they serve no lift function. But the trade-off—a simpler overall design—is part of Beta’s central strategy: Rather than applying for FAA certification for the more novel and complex eVTOL A250, with its multiple lift systems and transitions between hover and wing-borne flight, the company is bringing the runway version forward first. It’s certifying the motor, batteries, fly-by-wire controls, and much of the airframe in the simpler aircraft; it will then reuse evidence and manufacturing experience when it certifies the A250.The other big reason to start with the CX300 is logistical: The United States has more than 5,000 public-use airports, so the CX300 can operate from infrastructure that already exists. The cargo and medical flights that the aircraft will initially tackle offer tightly defined missions in which lower energy and maintenance costs matter more than cabin amenities. Hence the company’s decision to leave cabin air-conditioning off the CX300, at least for now. “Things that don’t exist on an airplane don’t weigh anything,” Clark says. “They can’t break. You don’t have to service them…. You don’t have to certify them.”“This strategy, at the beginning, was seen as a failure of their VTOL,” says analyst Sergio Cecutta, a managing partner at SMG Consulting. “But I think a lot of people were very myopic. It was a way for [Beta] to break up the certification in two pieces, and so they have retired a lot of the risk. [A majority] of the [CX300’s] parts are shared with the VTOL, and that means that a lot of the documentation for certification can be easily reused on the VTOL side when that certification starts.”How Redundancy Enhances SafetyA decade and a half after electric cars began making significant inroads into the automotive market, electric airplane technology is advancing rapidly. In some ways, an electric car has more complicated power-engineering requirements than an electric aircraft. An electric car’s power demands change constantly, while an electric aircraft operates in a narrower set of power regimes: takeoff, level flight, and descent.But if your car’s drivetrain fails, you can just pull over to the side of the road. That’s not an option for a pilot 1,000 meters up. The hurdle isn’t that electric motors are inherently fragile; it’s that aviation safety standards are extraordinarily demanding. Catastrophic failure conditions generally must be held to roughly one chance in a billion flight hours or less.The two versions of Beta’s Alia aircraft—the A250 [shown] and the CX300—share many of the same components, which the company believes will speed up FAA certification.Beta TechnologiesBecause individual electrical components cannot meet that standard on their own, electric aircraft rely on layered redundancy: duplicate power paths, independent inverters, fault isolation, monitoring, and backup capacity. Each layer helps close the safety gap, but each also adds weight and complexity.To close the reliability gap and satisfy regulators who have little experience with electric aircraft, Beta engineers have built redundancy into the motor itself. When I visit the company’s motor shop, technicians are assembling the H500A pusher motor, which splits up critical components like the inverters and stator sections.“You could lose an entire half of the system, and it would still function the same,” says Adam Lowe, Beta’s flight operations lead.The H500A’s broad diameter gives the magnetic forces greater leverage, producing high torque at low rotational speed, a useful trait when turning large propellers. The H500A weighs about 75 kilograms and produces about 640 kilowatts of power. The motor achieves 97.5 percent efficiency, based on internal testing; typical conventional turboprops have an efficiency range of 25 to 35 percent.Beta had aimed to have its motor certified in the first half of 2026, a deadline that passed without a public announcement. Although the company’s certification strategy is aimed at streamlining the process, getting clearance to fly from the Federal Aviation Administration is probably still its biggest obstacle. For Beta, and any company trying to bring an electric aircraft into service, the FAA must sign off first on the propeller, then the motor, then the aircraft. After that, the manufacturer must pursue a production certificate from the FAA, proving that the company can build the same approved aircraft again and again, with the right tools, records, inspections, quality controls, supplier oversight, training, calibration, and parts tracking.Beta has found a narrow on-ramp to get the A250 flying before full certification. Under the FAA’s eVTOL Integration Pilot Program, companies and public agencies can run FAA-supervised, real-world trials to test how advanced air-mobility aircraft might operate in the national airspace. For Beta, that included July flights across a Virginia-to-Maryland corridor carrying, among other things, a manufactured transplantable organ in a medical containment system. Beta has also been chosen for six other FAA-backed trials.For the fixed-wing Alia CX300, customer trials are moving in parallel. In March, Beta’s CX300 successfully completed 23 flights over 10 days on Loganair routes linking six Scottish cities. The airline has since signed a term sheet for five Beta aircraft, with options for five more and service expected to start in 2029.Innovation in eVTOLsBeta is pushing so hard for the A250 because the case for electric flight gets even more interesting with vertical-lift aircraft. Helicopters and other VTOLs have a less orderly life than fixed-wing planes do. They must hover, sidestep, turn in place, settle, climb, and hold themselves in the air by main force. But conventional helicopters are built around a rotor speed that varies only a few percent because the engine, transmission, and rotor system are all designed around one narrow operating point.“One of the dreams of vertical flight has been to vary the rotor rpm,” says Anubhav Datta, an aerospace engineering professor at the University of Maryland, College Park, who studies electric propulsion and vertical flight. Electric motors, he argues, have advanced enough to make that practical. A rotor that can slow down or speed up as conditions change can waste less energy, make less noise, and better match the odd assortment of tasks that vertical flight demands.Electric propulsion also lets designers break up the work with multiple motors. The weight of an electric motor is tied closely to its torque. If you spread that torque among several motors, each of the motors can shrink, meaning that their combined weight is less than would be needed for one big engine. This is one reason why so many electric aircraft designs appear to have sprouted propellers wherever an engineer found a place to put one.There is a downside to using multiple smaller motors: If one of the motors quits, the rotor it’s driving has little stored energy compared to the inertia in a conventional helicopter’s one large rotor. This inertia can come in handy if the engine dies, because the rotor will continue to spin and allow the pilot to descend under control, in an emergency technique known as autorotation. Beta Technologies, Made in Vermont Beta TechnologiesAccording to Beta Technologies founder and CEO Kyle Clark, his native Vermont—known more for its maple syrup and outdoorsy lifestyle than its technological achievements—is a big part of the secret to Beta’s success at attracting talent. “The recruiting strategy is this: You find anybody who has vacationed in Vermont or went to school here. And you say, ‘You can actually live here and have a high-paying job in a mission-driven business, doing something that you care about with great people.’ ” The company currently employs about 1,500 people, most of them in South Burlington, Vt., and is actively hiring for its engineering, manufacturing, software, flight operations, and commercial teams.As befits an aviation company, Beta offers flying lessons for all employees—a perk that is intended to give engineers, software developers, pilots, and mechanics a common language. The company also operates a health clinic for employees and their families and is building a day care center. Those programs have an idealistic Vermont flavor that is shaped by Clark and his wife, Katie Clark, who is Beta’s head of brand, creative, culture and spaces. But they also serve a practical purpose: attracting and retaining the best people in a small labor market. —T.C.

The top three U.S. electric-aircraft makers are taking different approaches to the same challenge: how to make short-hop flight cleaner, quieter, and commercially useful.

Kyle Clark, a native of Vermont, founded Beta in 2017 to realize his long-held dream of building a hybrid-electric aircraft. (For his bachelor’s thesis at Harvard University, he designed a flight-control system for such an aircraft.) Martine Rothblatt, CEO of the biotech company United Therapeutics and cofounder of SiriusXM satellite radio, invested $48 million in Beta to fund its first prototype, with the eventual goal of using the aircraft to transport organs for transplantation.

The company has kept much of its design and manufacturing in-house, partly because the pieces are closely coupled and partly because suitable aviation components didn’t exist when development began. Vertical integration gives Beta’s engineers control over the interfaces and any failure data. But it also means the company must be competent at several businesses simultaneously: airframes, electric motors, inverters, batteries, software, chargers, and aircraft production.

The production line can build both the fixed-wing CX300 and the vertical-lift A250, which share the same fuselage, wings, pusher motor (for horizontal propulsion), batteries, avionics, and much of the tooling. This results in some one-size-fits-all decisions: The CX300’s twin booms, which connect the wing to the tail surfaces, look superfluous until you see the A250’s four lift propellers mounted on similar structures.

On the CX300, those booms add weight and drag even though they serve no lift function. But the trade-off—a simpler overall design—is part of Beta’s central strategy: Rather than applying for FAA certification for the more novel and complex eVTOL A250, with its multiple lift systems and transitions between hover and wing-borne flight, the company is bringing the runway version forward first. It’s certifying the motor, batteries, fly-by-wire controls, and much of the airframe in the simpler aircraft; it will then reuse evidence and manufacturing experience when it certifies the A250.

The other big reason to start with the CX300 is logistical: The United States has more than 5,000 public-use airports, so the CX300 can operate from infrastructure that already exists. The cargo and medical flights that the aircraft will initially tackle offer tightly defined missions in which lower energy and maintenance costs matter more than cabin amenities. Hence the company’s decision to leave cabin air-conditioning off the CX300, at least for now. “Things that don’t exist on an airplane don’t weigh anything,” Clark says. “They can’t break. You don’t have to service them…. You don’t have to certify them.”

“This strategy, at the beginning, was seen as a failure of their VTOL,” says analyst Sergio Cecutta, a managing partner at SMG Consulting. “But I think a lot of people were very myopic. It was a way for [Beta] to break up the certification in two pieces, and so they have retired a lot of the risk. [A majority] of the [CX300’s] parts are shared with the VTOL, and that means that a lot of the documentation for certification can be easily reused on the VTOL side when that certification starts.”

A decade and a half after electric cars began making significant inroads into the automotive market, electric airplane technology is advancing rapidly. In some ways, an electric car has more complicated power-engineering requirements than an electric aircraft. An electric car’s power demands change constantly, while an electric aircraft operates in a narrower set of power regimes: takeoff, level flight, and descent.

But if your car’s drivetrain fails, you can just pull over to the side of the road. That’s not an option for a pilot 1,000 meters up. The hurdle isn’t that electric motors are inherently fragile; it’s that aviation safety standards are extraordinarily demanding. Catastrophic failure conditions generally must be held to roughly one chance in a billion flight hours or less.

The two versions of Beta’s Alia aircraft—the A250 [shown] and the CX300—share many of the same components, which the company believes will speed up FAA certification.

Because individual electrical components cannot meet that standard on their own, electric aircraft rely on layered redundancy: duplicate power paths, independent inverters, fault isolation, monitoring, and backup capacity. Each layer helps close the safety gap, but each also adds weight and complexity.

To close the reliability gap and satisfy regulators who have little experience with electric aircraft, Beta engineers have built redundancy into the motor itself. When I visit the company’s motor shop, technicians are assembling the H500A pusher motor, which splits up critical components like the inverters and stator sections.

“You could lose an entire half of the system, and it would still function the same,” says Adam Lowe, Beta’s flight operations lead.

The H500A’s broad diameter gives the magnetic forces greater leverage, producing high torque at low rotational speed, a useful trait when turning large propellers. The H500A weighs about 75 kilograms and produces about 640 kilowatts of power. The motor achieves 97.5 percent efficiency, based on internal testing; typical conventional turboprops have an efficiency range of 25 to 35 percent.

Beta had aimed to have its motor certified in the first half of 2026, a deadline that passed without a public announcement. Although the company’s certification strategy is aimed at streamlining the process, getting clearance to fly from the Federal Aviation Administration is probably still its biggest obstacle. For Beta, and any company trying to bring an electric aircraft into service, the FAA must sign off first on the propeller, then the motor, then the aircraft. After that, the manufacturer must pursue a production certificate from the FAA, proving that the company can build the same approved aircraft again and again, with the right tools, records, inspections, quality controls, supplier oversight, training, calibration, and parts tracking.

Beta has found a narrow on-ramp to get the A250 flying before full certification. Under the FAA’s eVTOL Integration Pilot Program, companies and public agencies can run FAA-supervised, real-world trials to test how advanced air-mobility aircraft might operate in the national airspace. For Beta, that included July flights across a Virginia-to-Maryland corridor carrying, among other things, a manufactured transplantable organ in a medical containment system. Beta has also been chosen for six other FAA-backed trials.

For the fixed-wing Alia CX300, customer trials are moving in parallel. In March, Beta’s CX300 successfully completed 23 flights over 10 days on Loganair routes linking six Scottish cities. The airline has since signed a term sheet for five Beta aircraft, with options for five more and service expected to start in 2029.

Beta is pushing so hard for the A250 because the case for electric flight gets even more interesting with vertical-lift aircraft. Helicopters and other VTOLs have a less orderly life than fixed-wing planes do. They must hover, sidestep, turn in place, settle, climb, and hold themselves in the air by main force. But conventional helicopters are built around a rotor speed that varies only a few percent because the engine, transmission, and rotor system are all designed around one narrow operating point.

“One of the dreams of vertical flight has been to vary the rotor rpm,” says Anubhav Datta, an aerospace engineering professor at the University of Maryland, College Park, who studies electric propulsion and vertical flight. Electric motors, he argues, have advanced enough to make that practical. A rotor that can slow down or speed up as conditions change can waste less energy, make less noise, and better match the odd assortment of tasks that vertical flight demands.

Electric propulsion also lets designers break up the work with multiple motors. The weight of an electric motor is tied closely to its torque. If you spread that torque among several motors, each of the motors can shrink, meaning that their combined weight is less than would be needed for one big engine. This is one reason why so many electric aircraft designs appear to have sprouted propellers wherever an engineer found a place to put one.

There is a downside to using multiple smaller motors: If one of the motors quits, the rotor it’s driving has little stored energy compared to the inertia in a conventional helicopter’s one large rotor. This inertia can come in handy if the engine dies, because the rotor will continue to spin and allow the pilot to descend under control, in an emergency technique known as autorotation.

According to Beta Technologies founder and CEO Kyle Clark, his native Vermont—known more for its maple syrup and outdoorsy lifestyle than its technological achievements—is a big part of the secret to Beta’s success at attracting talent. “The recruiting strategy is this: You find anybody who has vacationed in Vermont or went to school here. And you say, ‘You can actually live here and have a high-paying job in a mission-driven business, doing something that you care about with great people.’ ” The company currently employs about 1,500 people, most of them in South Burlington, Vt., and is actively hiring for its engineering, manufacturing, software, flight operations, and commercial teams.

As befits an aviation company, Beta offers flying lessons for all employees—a perk that is intended to give engineers, software developers, pilots, and mechanics a common language. The company also operates a health clinic for employees and their families and is building a day care center. Those programs have an idealistic Vermont flavor that is shaped by Clark and his wife, Katie Clark, who is Beta’s head of brand, creative, culture and spaces. But they also serve a practical purpose: attracting and retaining the best people in a small labor market. —T.C.

Electric-aircraft makers compensate with redundant motors and electrical channels, but most leading designs also have wings to allow gliding. In Beta’s A250, that means that if all else fails, the pilot still has a shot at reaching a suitable landing spot.

That helps explain the convergence in today’s eVTOL designs. Joby Aviation’s S4 uses six propellers that tilt between lift and cruise. Archer Aviation’s Midnight has six tilting propellers and six dedicated lift props. Beta’s A250 keeps its four lift propellers horizontal and uses a separate pusher motor for forward flight.

In Beta’s home state of Vermont, the electricity produced in the state comes almost entirely from renewable sources. Of course, electric aviation’s climate advantage depends not only on how clean the power grid is, but on how efficiently the aircraft uses energy. The turbines in conventional jet engines are marvelous machines, but they throw away a lot of energy as heat. Plus they need to carry the hardware to manage that heat—inlets, ducts, radiators, oil coolers—and the drag that comes with pulling air through all of it.

Kyle Clark [in cockpit], founder and CEO of Beta Technologies, pilots the company’s Alia CX300, an electric plane that Beta expects to receive FAA certification in 2027. Clark began designing a hybrid electric plane while still in college.Beta Technologies

By contrast, an electric design, with no combustion, gearbox, or oil circuits, avoids much of that energy burden. The result is an aircraft that is more than twice as “slippery” in terms of its lift-to-drag ratio. Batteries will never come close to the fuel density of petroleum fuels. But because electric drivetrains are much more efficient than combustion engines, a battery doesn’t necessarily need to match the energy density of petroleum fuel to be useful.

While the motors on an electric aircraft provide the drama at takeoff and landing, the batteries set nearly every boundary: the range, payload, charge time, cooling load, reserve energy, and, ultimately, useful life. For now, the hardest limit on electric aviation is not imagination, software, or even motors. It is the stubborn weight of stored energy.

“One thing you notice about [electric] airplanes is that they are all small,” says Cecutta, the aviation analyst. “They are not small because someone has done a market study and decided that is the optimal size. They are small because you cannot make them big. This limitation is from the batteries.”

In a conventional aircraft, fuel accounts for a significant percentage of the takeoff weight. But that weight gradually disappears during flight. In an electric plane, the battery packs also account for a lot of the weight; in Beta’s CX300, they’re close to half the aircraft’s mass. But a drained battery at landing weighs the same as a fully charged one at takeoff. That means the rest of the aircraft must be light enough to lift a power source that never gets lighter, yet strong enough to endure vibration, turbulence, hard landings, and years of fatigue. The more engineers push to save weight, Datta notes, the more flexible the airframe becomes, making the wings and other structures more prone to bending, twisting, and vibration. In that sense, the battery problem becomes a structural problem, too.

Beta’s CX300 carries five liquid-cooled battery packs of about 45 kilowatt-hours each, giving the aircraft roughly 225 kWh of rated capacity, though not all of that may be usable in flight. Each pack weighs 255 kg and contains 2,772 cylindrical cells. Altogether, about 14,000 cells sit beneath the occupied cabin, each one a tiny storehouse of energy, heat, and potential trouble.

The burden of the battery explains much of what I see elsewhere in Beta’s aircraft: the 15-meter wingspan, the composite structure, and the almost doctrinal effort to leave out every piece of hardware and creature comfort that’s not essential to the mission. The CX300 even does without flaps, which most aircraft use to generate extra lift at low speeds, so it must take off and land unusually fast for an aircraft of its size.

On Beta’s battery line, commercially supplied standard 21-millimeter-diameter, 70-mm-long cells are tested and assembled. Cells become modules, modules become subpacks, and eleven subpacks become a 45-kWh pack. Cooling plates manage heat during normal operation, while passive barriers and electrical isolation are designed to contain the ugly edge cases that Beta’s testing process intentionally triggers.

The modular design of the packs will give Beta’s planes a path to better batteries as the cells and battery chemistry improve. Caputo tells me that the company is already evaluating higher-density cells at its battery testing facility in nearby St. Albans, Vt. More watt-hours per kilogram could eventually mean more range, more payload, or both.

What sets Beta Technologies apart from its competitors is the breadth of its ambitions: It’s not just building electric aircraft. It’s also cultivating a broader ecosystem to nurture the growth of electric aviation.

For instance, Beta intends its motor business to extend beyond its own aircraft. The company has delivered lift motors for Eve Air Mobility’s proof-of-concept aircraft and has a contract to supply up to $1 billion in pusher motors as Eve’s program develops. In addition, Beta is selling flight-control computers and software to Horizon Aircraft. It is also producing propulsion systems for the military, as well as the MV250, a hybrid-electric autonomous aircraft for defense missions.

Beta’s fast-charging stations [left] have been installed at 138 locations. The thermal-management station [right] keeps the battery from overheating during charging. The company uses the open-standard Combined Charging System (CCS) so that other aircraft can use its chargers. Beta Technologies

Further evidence of Beta’s far-ranging plans is visible near the battery line in the South Burlington facility, where workers are readying a few of the company’s 1.5-meter-tall Charge Cubes for shipping. Intended for installation at airports, each cube takes 480-volt AC from the grid and can deliver up to 320 kW at as much as 1,000 volts DC. According to Beta, charging either model of its Alia aircraft takes about 35 minutes, from the minimum charge required under visual flight rules to full state. A separate thermal-management cube circulates water-glycol coolant through the aircraft to keep the batteries from overheating during charging.

The charge cubes use the Combined Charging System (CCS), an open standard increasingly shared across the electric transportation industry. Beta’s strategy resembles Tesla’s decision to treat charging as part of the vehicle business, with one important difference: Beta is courting other aircraft makers, airports, and operators to use its technology. Archer, Atlantic Aviation, Signature Aviation, and government agencies have purchased or installed Beta’s charging equipment. As of October, Beta has 138 fast-charge locations, with additional sites in permitting or construction.

For charging beyond the fixed network, Beta offers a wheeled 40-kW Mini Cube and a 65-kW Mini Cube+ that can ride inside an Alia and plug into more modest electrical service. The Mini Cube can also charge cars, buses, and trucks—any vehicle that uses the CCS standard.

Back above Lake Champlain, the CX300 levels off, and the motor recedes to a faint hum. Then Caputo switches it off, and there’s nothing but the rush of wind around the canopy. The aircraft glides on its long wings while the coasting propeller turns the motor into a generator, feeding about 9 kW back to the battery.

Then we turn to the east and slip into the landing queue behind a National Guard C-130 Hercules, and Caputo powers and slides the throttle forward. The motor springs to life again, pushing the airplane, and perhaps aviation itself, into the future.

Tom Clynes, an IEEE Spectrum contributing editor, is a writer and photojournalist who covers science and the environment.

#405. Kengo Kuma & Associates’ Water Culture House to Open in Copenhagen on December 5, 2026

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Designed by Kengo Kuma & Associates in collaboration with Cornelius Vöge Atelier for Architecture and Vilhelm Lauritzen Architects, KAI Water Culture House is set to open on December 5, 2026, on Papirøen (Paper Island) in Copenhagen, Denmark. The 5,800 m² public facility combines ten indoor and outdoor pools, wellness facilities, cultural spaces, and access to Copenhagen Harbour. Comprising five brick towers above a glazed base, the project brings together swimming, recreation, and cultural programming as part of the ongoing transformation of the city's waterfront.

Designed by Kengo Kuma & Associates in collaboration with Cornelius Vöge Atelier for Architecture and Vilhelm Lauritzen Architects, KAI Water Culture House is set to open on December 5, 2026, on Papirøen (Paper Island) in Copenhagen, Denmark. The building's five brick towers are shaped in relation to the pools beneath them, creating distinct interior spaces while establishing a varied roofline. Constructed using approximately 300,000 handmade bricks, the towers contrast with the glazed lower structure, where windows frame views of the harbour and bring natural light into the building. A central feature is the Valley Pool, an outdoor pool positioned between the towers on the first floor, allowing visitors to swim under open skies. An outdoor onsen pool on the same level overlooks the harbour, extending the relationship between the building's interior programme and its waterfront setting.

The facility also incorporates three saunas, a steam bath, and a heated stone. Beyond the building, designated swimming and dipping zones are planned along a timber boardwalk, with views toward the Royal Danish Playhouse and Opera Park. The project originated in 2015, when the City of Copenhagen invited residents to submit ideas for a cultural and leisure facility on Paper Island. #408. Obra Blanca 2026 Explores the Connection Between Materials and Design

#348. Spokane defends City Council resolution naming politician-turned-pastor as ‘domestic terrorist’

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A lower court found the Spokane City Council must face a lawsuit from former state Representative Matthew Shea over a resolution condemning the mayor's participation at an event with the right-winger.

SEATTLE (CN) â The city of Spokane appeared before the Ninth Circuit on Wednesday seeking to dodge a former Washington state representative’s claim that the City Council violated the state constitution by labeling him a domestic terrorist.

âA legislative resolution does not inflict punishment within the meaning of the bill of attainder clauses simply because it criticizes a public official where it imposes no affirmative disability or restraint in any way on anyone,â

In 2019, an investigation by the Washington state House of Representatives found former state Representative Matthew Shea had engaged in domestic terrorism by supporting separatists’ plots of militia groups seeking to create a white homeland in Washington and Idaho. It also found he endorsed killing all males who didnât agree to follow fundamentalist Biblical law. He was expelled and left office in 2021.

In 2023, Shea appeared at a large Christian revival event in Spokane, where then-Mayor Nadine Woodward joined him on stage. To express its disappointment and disapproval of Woodwardâs participation in the event, the City Council adopted a resolution denouncing Woodward in which they describe Shea as an âidentified domestic terrorist.â

Shea sued Spokane and the four members of the City Council who voted for the resolution, arguing the action violated his First Amendment rights and amounted to an unlawful bill of attainder â or legislation that targets an individual or group, implicating them in a crime and imposing punishment without a judicial trial.

The lower court dismissed the bulk of Sheaâs claims, but held that he plausibly pleaded the resolution was a bill of attainder. The City Council appealed, arguing the lower court was correct in many ways but fell short by allowing the bill of attainder claim to proceed since the resolution didn’t impose any punishment on Shea.

An essential feature of whether a statute is a bill of attainder is whether it imposes a punishment such as death, imprisonment, banishment and property confiscation.

âEverything else the district court did was exactly correct: It rightly understood the resolution as ’toothless’ because it regulated nothing, prescribed nothing and compelled nothing,â argued Spokaneâs attorney, Zach Pekelis with Pacifica Law Group. âThe district court also recognized the resolution’s obvious nonpunitive purpose, namely, to express the council’s collective opinion that anti-LGBTQ rhetoric is antithetical to the city’s values.â

U.S. Circuit Judge Milan Smith interjected with a question.

âSo bottom line from the city’s perspective, there’s simply no punishment here, and because there’s no punishment, there’s no cause of action?â the George W. Bush appointee asked. The city agreed.

Shea argued he had effectively been banished under the resolution. While the lower court found the resolution contained no language amounting to banishment, it also concluded it âcannot say that there are no facts supporting plaintiffâs claims.â

The city argued the resolution had no such effect and served only a nonpunitive expressive purpose. It further suggested the City Council members are subject to qualified immunity.

Shea, on the other hand, argued the City Councilâs resolution was an âact of hostility toward religion,â as Shea had been leading a prayer at the event and argued the resolution did not need to target him.

âI was not being hyperbolic when I said that this is probably one of the most egregious sort of attainders in this country’s history,â said Sheaâs attorney Christopher Hogue. âThey sit there and declare him to be a criminal.â

Shea, who is a pastor, said all he was guilty of was âhaving a love for Jesus Christâ and was simply spreading that love through prayer at the event, which he co-organized with Sean Fuecht, who the City Council described as a âknown anti-LGBTQ extremist.â

Shea argued attainders historically involved reputational damage before more severe punishment.

âI think in old England, that’s what you do first before you go hang them in the gallows,â Hogue said. You make them a nobody; you take away their being, their standing, their reputation.â

Shea argued the resolution deprived his political and civil rights, as well of his right to petition, right to associate, and deprived him of his dignity.

âThe resolution literally says that he doesn’t belong in the city of Spokane, I mean theyâre exiling him,â Hogue said.

The Ninth Circuit panel â which also included U.S. Circuit Judge Morgan Christen, a Barack Obama appointee, and U.S. District Judge Sunshine Sykes, a Joe Biden appointee who sat on the panel by designation from the Central District of California â did not indicate when it would rule.

#299. The 2026 Unofficial Marathon Awards, According to an Editor Running New York City

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

This is the gear you actually need to make it to 26.2.

Last year, when I trained for my first marathon, I went a bit gear-crazy. I searched high and low for all the best products to help me get through training and across the finish line. And, I had a lot of fun doing it. I tested out dozens of sneakers, agonized over my race day outfit, and indulged in all the high-tech recovery gadgets (I have a recovery tool for basically every part of my body, from head to toe).

This year, my approach to gear is a lot simpler because I know what I like. More importantly, I know what works. For example: I’m going on two training cycles now of exclusively using Gu Raspberry Lemonade gels as my fuel on long runs. Yeah, when something is good, I’m dedicated.

So, while some of my training staple top spots haven’t budged—like my Bombas running socks, Garmin Forerunner 570, and Therabody JetBoots Pro Plus—that just means the things in this year’s Marathon Awards really impressed me.

I’m still loyal to many of the products in last year’s roundup of my faves, but these are the new additions that deserve a spot on the list.

I have been pretty dead-set on my Nike Vomero Plus (which I wore throughout training and on race day last year) as my max cushioned shoe for everyday runs for a while—but these sneaks from Hoka finally gave them some competition for that spot. They’re less pillowy and more structured, so I feel more in control on a run, and my heel and ankle have more support. During training this time around, I found that my feet started to crave a bit more of that stability from a shoe, so I switched over to the Cliftons to get me through my peak mileage. They hold their own on easy and long runs when it comes to comfort, and feel even better than the Nikes when I’m doing my speed intervals on the track.

There comes a point in training when I simply cannot get myself to wear anything but sneakers. My feet and body just need the support, even at work or running errands or going out with friends. The new Eliot Ryder from Tracksmith is a pillowy soft shoe that cushions every step. Made for running, it could totally double as your easy run go-to, but I’ve been living in it outside of logging miles as well. The shoe’s design also makes it a little more fashionable than other max-cushioned trainers that scream “running shoe.”

Recovery slides are definitely new for my training this year. After dealing with some mysterious foot aches and pains this time around, I started to wear slides around my apartment to avoid walking around flat-footed. Compared to others I tried, these are the softest and offer the most arch support.

I ran my favorite half marathon to date (the Lululemon SeaWheeze half) in this tank—so I’m a little sentimental. But still: it has the classic soft and breathable material I love Lululemon for, it’s super comfortable and lightweight over long distances, and comes in bright colors that will get you spotted on the course. I’m very much considering ironing my name onto the one I have and wearing it on race day.

I trained during the summer months in New York City, so a lightweight top was critical. This one from Salomon kept me comfortable throughout training (and also has a really cool, elevated look). I cannot stress enough just how lightweight, breathable, and quick-drying this fabric is. Truly a gem of a find!

Now that the weather is cooling down, this top from Rhone is my favorite for an early fall day. The material is soft and breathable, making it a perfect transition layer. And, I think the crop and mesh design make it cuter than other running long sleeves.

I would say the biggest thing I didn’t nail down last year was a running vest, so I’m excited to share that I’ve got one that I loved this time around. The big problem with last year’s (unnamed) vest is that the water bottles were always popping out. They stay put in this one from Hoka, which also feels lightweight and easily stores everything I need to bring with me. The closures are easy to work, it feels really secure and comfortable across long distances, and I like the pop the purple gives my usually all-black running outfit.

When all my laundry is clean, these are the first pair of shorts I grab above every other option. The main reason: I want to be comfortable on a run, and these are the most comfortable shorts I have by far. They don’t ride up, provide compression—but not too much compression—and have a softer feel. They have two pockets along the back (one zippered, where I keep my keys) and a mesh one (where I throw my empty gels), plus one on the right thigh (where I keep my phone).

This pair from Bandit also hits all the important notes in running shorts: they don’t ride up, offer compression, and are comfortable even on double-digit mile runs. These shorts also have extra grippy material inside to help them stay put and have pockets on both thighs and two along the back center, which is helpful if you have more things to carry with you on a run. (Come race day, when I don’t plan to wear a vest because I won’t need to carry my own water supply, I’m going to be happy to have an extra pocket to hold my gels!)

Lululemon’s low-impact Flow Y bra got an upgrade to be more supportive for higher-impact activities. The racerback bra has a mesh panel along the back too, making it more breathable on hot days. While training through the summer, I mostly end up running in just a sports bra, and this one also has a fun pattern to show off.

It’s very hard to successfully cross a finish line without the help of a training program. This year, I worked with Kelly Roberts, an RRCA-certified run coach and creator of the Badass Lady Gang. It’s a run community, and now, an app where you can get customized training programs, connect with Kelly and other runners, and utilize strength training, fueling, mindset, and recovery resources. It’s the most thorough platform I’ve ever seen, and Kelly was there to help me navigate the highs (5 and 10K PRs when I wasn’t even trying) and lows (strep throat) that popped up during training.

Bose just dropped these earbuds and I’ve been obsessed ever since I got my hands on them. They stay secure on your ears and have that open ear feature so you can still hear everything around you. But, since they’re Bose, the sound also feels more high quality than other options, and they’re slightly louder for moments when you really need to pump those tunes.

I love these sunglasses from Warby Parker Sport. They’re lightweight, but still feel like a solid, nice pair of sunglasses, and their look feels a little more elevated than some of the aggressively big and bold sunglasses that runners have to choose from. They also stay on my face, rain, shine, or sweat.

Not to be dramatic, but these hair ties from Goody changed my life. Last year, I watched a bunch of tutorials of how different influencers styled their hair for a race and purchased different products that would hopefully help tame my flyaways and often-drooping ponytail to no avail. It turns out, all I needed were these hair ties. They’re thicker and stronger than the regular old ones I’ve been wearing since I was a kid, and seem impossible to break. I can run for miles now and my hair doesn’t budge. I don’t know what kind of magic is in these things, but I’m grateful.

I don’t love the way most sunscreens sit on my skin, but obviously, some SPF is important when you’re spending that much time in the sun. I actually like this sunscreen from Summer Fridays—it melts into my skin, keeps me covered, and doesn’t get into my eyes when I’m on a run.

If you’re acne-prone like me, all that running and sweating that comes with marathon training could do a number on your skin. This year, I got in the habit of immediately rinsing my face with water once I got back to my apartment and then spraying it with Tower 28’s SOS spray for good measure. So far, no major breakouts to report.

She reports, writes, and edits content ranging from service stories to in-depth features and awards packages across all verticals, including health, fitness, sports, and beauty. While at WH, she’s traveled to Paris, France to watch Faith Kipyegon attempt a sub-4-minute mile and covered major races like Ultra Trail du Mont Blanc in Chamonix. Olivia is a RRCA-certified running coach who is especially passionate about helping people start their running journey and is training for her second marathon. #354. Manhattan’s Lost Its Screen Presence

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The other weekend, I pulled up Woody Allen’s Manhattan (1979) on YouTube because I’ve been starved for the highbrow and the funny, a rare marriage that movies used to achieve with some regularity, but now hardly ever bother to attempt. 

Let’s disregard the plot, here, and the characters, for there is a great deal about both that resists easy or simple discussion in the present American climate, and focus instead on the surface: the look of the film, the feel of it, the particular New York it conjures and holds up to the light like a jeweler with a loupe.

My feeling for New York City has always come in a peculiar Southern blend of love and affliction. I don’t care for the rudeness, or the flat, clipped music of the accent, the way a sentence up there lands like a screen door.

But some years ago—high school, through college, on into my early twenties—my family made a yearly trip north around Christmas, either before the holiday or just after it. Mother, father, sister, brother, and myself. We took rooms near Times Square. We saw the shows. We ate at the restaurants with the heavy menus and the heavier check.

My mother and sister went shopping. The first year, my father, brother, and I made the mistake of going along, and we stood there in Macy’s—the famous one—waiting among the racks like the accused waiting outside a courtroom, until finally we drifted, defeated, to the section where they sell the beds.

And there, without much discussion, the three of us climbed up onto the display mattresses and took our naps in full view of the shoppers, three Southern males asleep in a snowdrift of goose-down pillows while New Yorkers and tourists alike rushed past us toward their purchases. We never went shopping with the women again.

Manhattan opens with a voice reciting its own first sentence, over and over, dissatisfied, revising itself in real time: “Chapter One. He adored New York City. He idolized it all out of proportion…” What we are watching, before a single frame of story unspools, is the composition of New York, its narration into existence by a man who admits, within the first 30 seconds, that his version of it is a romance rather than a record.

Isaac Davis, played by Allen, drafts Manhattan, discards the draft, drafts it again, each revision a further layer of gloss over a city that by the film’s own admission was never quite there to begin with. Gershwin swells. The skyline assembles itself in black-and-white, denuded of the actual chromatic clutter of a real metropolis: no exhaust-brown, no bodega-awning red, no garbage-day green.

The result is not less than New York, but more: a hyperreal locus, a city more New York than New York, purified into its own advertisement. By the time the narration settles on its final version of the sentence, we have watched a myth being built in front of us, brick by adoring brick, and we have watched it happen with the confidence of a myth that knows it will outlast its subject.

Consider, in this light, why the film insists on monochrome: color would have been the honest choice, but the lesser one. The black-and-white suggests a setting already understood as the past. Allen may have shot it that way because that’s how he remembered the city from boyhood. The image does not become nostalgia, but arrives already nostalgic, already a simulation of an image of a city, twice removed from the referent before the referent has even appeared.

The formal choices reward the kind of patient gazing that asks not what a shot means, but what it does: how it organizes the eye, how it withholds and releases. Willis’s camera holds wide and still. And it is Allen—small, voluble, anxious—who is made to cross the frame, dwarfed by the buildings on either side. The city is never made to accommodate the man. The compositions insist on this dynamic with a kind of moral clarity: architecture as fixed, permanent, indifferent; the human figure as transient, comic, and a little absurd against so much stone. There’s no accident in the fact that the funniest lines are often delivered in the widest shots: the humor is small; the buildings aren’t.

The bench scene deserves a word of correction before anything else, since even devoted admirers of the film misremember it: the silhouette behind Isaac and Mary at dawn is not the Brooklyn Bridge, but the Queensboro—the 59th Street Bridge, the one Simon and Garfunkel made feel groovy—shot from a little park at Sutton Place, the bench itself (according to my Internet searches) trucked in by the crew for that single image and trucked out again after.

Which is, in its own way, the whole method of the film in miniature: even the most iconic, most seemingly eternal image of New York on offer here was a temporary prop set down in front of a bridge for one morning’s shoot. The permanence is manufactured.

>>> Mamdani’s $22 Billion Housing Lunacy Proposal Will Socialize the Skyline

The film’s two registers—the melancholy of the story and the euphoria of the imagery—do not cancel each other out so much as run on parallel tracks, and the gap between those tracks is where the picture does its real work. Consider the function of the montage sequences against that of the dialogue scenes. The montages are non-diegetic in feeling even when diegetically grounded: they exist to establish a rhythm of civic wonder, asking nothing of the viewer but admiration.

The dialogue scenes, by contrast, are shot with almost no cutting at all: long, static two-shots, characters talking past each other in real time, the visual monotony forcing attention onto the halting, self-undermining texture of what is actually being said. The film is, formally speaking, telling us two different things simultaneously and daring us to notice the seam: the city as sublime, continuous, orchestral; the people within it as fragmented, hesitant, unable to complete a sentence about their own feelings without doubling back on it, exactly as the opening narration doubled back on itself.

The visual grandeur is never undercut by irony—Allen does not mock the skyline—but it is placed in a kind of formal tension with human smallness that neither element resolves. That unresolved tension, held shot after shot without comment, is the melancholy, and it’s built into the cutting pattern before it is ever spoken aloud.

Now, alas, to the present city—the real one—as it stands to be reorganized, and to the politics of the mayor presently promising to reorganize it.

New York has been bleeding people for years. The arithmetic of it is no longer a matter of dispute: the city and state have shed net domestic migrants every year for over a decade, hundreds of thousands of taxpayers relocating to Florida, Texas, and the Carolinas, taking with them not just households, but the tax base itself, since a disproportionate share of those leaving sit at the top of the income ledger.

Economists have written this story many times over. They generally tell it thus: rent regulation suppressing housing supply, tax rates chasing away the earners who fund the very programs meant to help everyone else, business formation slowing as regulatory and labor costs climb, the tax burden left to redistribute itself across a shrinking and less mobile base.

It is, by now, a familiar and well-documented indictment, and further democratic-socialist experimentation of the kind Zohran Mamdani proposes (price controls, municipal enterprises, steeper taxation of capital) is likely only to accelerate a trend already well underway. What economists rarely pause to mention, in this careful accounting of bad economics, is the bad aesthetics: the fact that a shrinking, flattened, price-controlled city is not only a poorer city, but a less legible one, a city that stops producing the aspirational, ambitious textures that an environment needs to be worth writing a Manhattan about in the first place.

Rent freezes, city-owned grocery stores, free buses, government-run childcare, union-built housing pinned beneath the same frozen rents, higher taxation on the wealthy who have historically financed the city’s theater of distinction: this is a program that wants New York to stop signifying, to stop chasing visual excess, to level unnecessary beauty, and to disappear the glorified aesthetics that defined Manhattan (the film).

The free market is a machine for producing urban variety and diversity. It sorts neighborhoods into strata, forces competition among restaurants and shops and doormen and galleries to distinguish themselves, and out of that sorting comes precisely the texture Isaac Davis fell in love with: the specific bookstore, the specific delicatessen, the specific bench.

Price signals are semiotic: a rent freeze not only caps a number, but also removes the mechanism by which neighborhoods differentiate themselves through investment, turnover, and risk. A city-owned grocery store cannot, by definition, be a particular grocery store as Zabar’s is particular because its entire justification is that it’s the same everywhere, fungible, a public utility rather than a character.

>>> Mamdani’s “Tax First, Think Later” Budget Plan

Today’s Manhattan looks different from Allen’s black-and-white mise-en-scène partly because of the regulations governing its buildings: facade inspection mandates have wrapped whole blocks in sidewalk sheds for years at a time, and emissions caps are pushing owners to retrofit prewar towers with electric heat pumps, through-wall units, and rooftop equipment.

Universal childcare and free buses, worthy as they may sound to the ear of compassion, are administered goods, which, besides being economically harmful to the very communities they purport to help, do not accumulate the patina of specificity, tenacity, drive: the worn brass rail, the eccentric owner, the 40 years of small adjustments to changing taste. These details turn a mere service into a grand milieu that a person can photograph in black-and-white and mean something by it. You can’t rent-control your way to a Rizzoli’s.

The specific aesthetic New York that this film so lovingly constructs was the product of an economy that permitted radical unevenness, permitted some blocks to become Sutton Place and others to remain what they were. The old New York could be ugly in reality and beautiful in art because it left room for artists to look upward, to take its disorder and inequality and noise and transfigure them into myth. The danger of Mamdani’s New York is not merely that it becomes poorer or more regulated, but that it becomes aesthetically downward-looking, a city preoccupied with administering what is wrong with it rather than generating the beauty that makes people want to imagine it as something more.

A politics organized entirely around flattening unevenness in the name of universal provision will tend to produce a city whose surfaces mean less. Mamdani’s New York, should it fully arrive, promises a setting that is more equal and more affordable and, quite possibly, less quotable, a city no future Woody Allen would find himself compelled to open his picture with “Chapter one. Dissatisfied. Starting over.”Top of Form

Bottom of Form

Which brings me back to that Christmas Macy’s, my father asleep on a showroom mattress, three Southerners playing hooky from the shopping. I think that’s what I’m partially mourning when I mourn the New York of Manhattan: not the wealth, not even the skyline, but the specificity, the sense that the city was a collection of unique rooms rather than a portfolio of interchangeable services.

The people I know who have left for Texas, for Florida, for the redder counties of this reddening country, will tell you they left the taxes, the crime, the cost. But listen long enough, and what they are really grieving, underneath the ledger, is that the New York which could still surprise you is going, has been going for a while now, and no rent freeze, however kindly meant, will bring it back, because you cannot legislate a city into being irreplaceable. You can only leave it alone long enough, and love it hard enough, to let it become so on its own, the way, once, at dawn, on a borrowed bench under a borrowed bridge, it did.

#413. Claranet Portugal Headquarters / PROMONTORIO

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Text description provided by the architects. Claranet is an IT company providing network hosting and managed application services (Hybrid Cloud Hosting) to corporate clients across Europe and Brazil. The practice was invited to design its Portuguese headquarters on the site of a former military depot, itself built on the grounds of the 17th-century Grilas Sisters' convent. The convent survived the 1755 earthquake but not the 1834 extinction of the religious orders. It became state property and in 1897, was converted into military warehouses. After the 2016 Web Summit, the city rescued it to create Lisbon's International Innovation Hub, now a group of municipal concession buildings for tech-based services and creative industries.

Text description provided by the architects. Claranet is an IT company providing network hosting and managed application services (Hybrid Cloud Hosting) to corporate clients across Europe and Brazil. The practice was invited to design its Portuguese headquarters on the site of a former military depot, itself built on the grounds of the 17th-century Grilas Sisters' convent. The convent survived the 1755 earthquake but not the 1834 extinction of the religious orders. It became state property and in 1897, was converted into military warehouses. #114. Arsenal or Man City? Chelsea legend Joe Cole makes Premier League title prediction

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Joe Cole believes Manchester City will beat Arsenal to the title – but only if their appeal of the Premier League’s guilty verdict is successful.

An independent commission found Man City guilty of over 100 breaches of financial regulations between 2009 and 2018.

Manchester City, the dominant force in English football over the past decade, arranged ‘sham contracts’ and ‘relied on sham agreements to artificially inflate the club’s revenues and reduce its costs’.

Etihad Bosses confirmed they had appealed the decision at the start of the month, claiming the commission’s ruling contained ‘clear material errors of law, principle and fact and is unsafe’.

Sign up to our daily football newsletter, pick your team and get the latest news sent directly to your inbox every morning.

Man City also said it had filed a ‘comprehensive body of irrefutable evidence [that] exists in support of all of its positions’.

Manchester City have lost two of their five games since the guilty verdict, suffering their first Premier League defeat of the season on Saturday, losing 2-1 to Aston Villa.

Enzo Maresca’s side are still joint-top of the Premier League, though, boasting the exact same record as defending champions Arsenal after eight games.

The Gunners suffered a shock defeat 3-0 at Brighton before the international break but have bounced back with three successive league wins and a hard-fought 2-2 draw with Bayern Munich in the Champions League.

Former Chelsea and England midfielder Cole backed Manchester City to dethrone Arsenal before a ball was kicked and still thinks Maresca will lift the Premier League trophy, but only if the club’s appeal is successful.

Use AI to go deeper into the stories you care about – powered by Metro and trusted publications.

If that proves not to be the case, Cole believes Arsenal will clinch back-to-back Premier League titles, with every other club in the division still ‘catching up’.

Asked for his latest Premier League title prediction after the weekend results, Cole said on The Dressing Room podcast: ‘I think Arsenal.

‘If Man City’s lawyers go to work, they win the league. The lawyers are the most important people in this right now! If not it’s Arsenal and everyone else is just catching up.’

Liverpool legend and ex-England defender Jamie Carragher also views Arsenal as favourites to win the title given the uncertainty at Manchester City.

‘We’ve said before that Arsenal are not the most exciting champions we’ve ever seen,’ Carragher told Sky Sports. ‘They still lack stardust in the final third and didn’t get that superstar over the line that they wanted in the summer.

‘There was talk of Vinicius Junior coming in obviously and Julian Alvarez but they didn’t get those deals done. They do lack star power in the final third and we saw more evidence of that on Sunday.

‘Some people will also say that Mikel Arteta has still got the reins on and is stopping them from showing their absolute best going forward because they value the defence so much.

‘But they’ve got the best squad in the country and the best starting line-up, I don’t think anyone can go up against that really except maybe Man City.

‘We said all of this last year as well, they didn’t have a game-changer but what they had is two top-quality players in every position.

‘I still see Arsenal as the team to stop. We obviously don’t know what’s going to happen with City, we have to take it as it is right now.’

Arsenal’s next Premier League game is a daunting trip to Liverpool on Sunday, while Man City host in-form Brighton the day before.

#366. Container Engineer at Supabase

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth. More than 9 million developers and 250,000+ customers now build on Supabase, and the majority of new databases on the platform are created by AI app builders — so the systems underneath have to stay reliable while they grow fast.

Container Engineering (ConEng) owns the internal container platform that runs Supabase's multi-tenant, multi-region services — the managed Kubernetes platform that other engineering teams build and deploy on top of. We own cluster lifecycle and cluster classes, multi-cluster and multi-region topology, capacity, scheduling, isolation and runtime safety, the higher-level platform APIs, the Kubernetes deployment contract and Argo semantics, rollout primitives, and runtime configuration and secret injection.

Your job is to make the platform itself trustworthy, observable, and cheap to operate at scale — and to raise the reliability bar for everyone who depends on it. This is not a gatekeeper role. You'll make the reliable path the easy path: building the automation and platform APIs that let product teams run safely by default, instrumenting what we operate, and making sure that when something breaks we detect it and recover fast.

Tooling & ecosystem — deep knowledge of the container and orchestration landscape (Kubernetes, containerd/OCI, the CNCF ecosystem) and a clear point of view on how to compose it.

Software engineering — designing and building the platform's automation, controllers, and internal tooling as production code, including solutions that don't come pre-shaped by an existing tool.

Operational / SRE practice — the discipline (SLOs, error budgets, incident response, ORRs) that runs the platform at scale as a predictable one.

We expect you to be genuinely deep in at least one of these, and many of our strongest candidates are deep in two (usually leaning toward one). Bringing real strength across all three is rare and exceptional — and even then, no one is equally strong in all three at once, so you'd focus where you're most needed at any given time. Different profiles are welcome here; tell us which of these is your center of gravity.

Regardless of the skill set, all team members carry a share of the team's on-call rota, responding to platform incidents and driving them to resolution and follow-up.

Define SLIs/SLOs and error budgets for the container platform itself, and build error-budget policies that actually gate platform changes.

Own the Operational Readiness Review (ORR) process for new platform capabilities and major changes.

Design and track metrics for platform uptime, and increase observability into the platform by capturing the right metrics, logs, and traces across the platform layers.

Drive the incident-to-improvement pipeline: facilitate blameless postmortems, connect findings to readiness gaps, spot repeat failure patterns, and push through systemic fixes.

Build and maintain the platform's automation, controllers, operators, and internal tooling — writing production code and shipping it, not just wiring together existing tools.

Design and implement the platform-side CI/CD and rollout primitives that speed up platform deployments while working within proper change and release management.

Implement and maintain security automation for the platform: intrusion detection, automated remediation, and patch management.

Identify and quantify operational toil, and build the automation that removes it rather than just documenting it.

Contribute to the platform's continual testing automation — the test suites and pipelines that validate platform releases are fit for purpose before they reach tenants.

Act as a platform reliability and architecture expert — contribute to failure-mode analysis, dependency mapping, and resilience design for multi-tenant container workloads.

Evaluate and evolve the platform's tooling ecosystem (orchestration, runtime, networking, isolation, and observability stack) with a clear, opinionated view on trade-offs.

Improve system architecture to reduce infrastructure cost while balancing security, reliability, and performance across tenants.

Guide teams building on the platform to design sustainable on-call practices for their own services: alert quality, escalation paths, runbook coverage, and noise reduction.

Have 5+ years in platform engineering, container/Kubernetes platform operations, SRE, or production infrastructure, and have operated production systems at scale and carried on-call for them.

Are genuinely deep in at least one — ideally two — of the three strength areas below (and tell us which is your center of gravity):

Tooling & ecosystem — know the container/orchestration landscape well: Kubernetes, containerd and the OCI/CRI standards, and the surrounding CNCF ecosystem (e.g. Argo CD/Argo Rollouts for GitOps and progressive delivery; Cilium/eBPF networking and the Gateway API; Karpenter/KEDA-style autoscaling; and workload-isolation runtimes such as gVisor or Kata Containers). You reason about trade-offs rather than reaching for defaults.

Software engineering — write real production code (Go and/or a comparable systems language) and can build controllers, operators, and platform APIs — stepping back to the underlying problem and building the right solution when no off-the-shelf tool fits.

Operational / SRE practice — are fluent in SLOs, error budgets, and operational KPIs; have led incident response and blameless postmortems; and have driven down MTTD/MTTR for systems you operated.

Have worked with large-scale multi-tenant systems and understand isolation as a spectrum — from namespaces, RBAC, network policies, and resource quotas through to stronger primitives such as virtual clusters (e.g. vCluster) or VM-class runtimes.

Operate confidently on AWS (multiple accounts, IAM, VPC) and are comfortable with infrastructure-as-code (Pulumi preferred; Terraform/CDK also fine) and Kubernetes.

Script and automate to eliminate toil rather than absorb it.

Communicate clearly with both infrastructure specialists and product engineers, and thrive in async, globally distributed teams.

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and iterating toward better systems over time.

Experience building and operating a multi-tenant Kubernetes platform that internal teams deploy onto.

Familiarity with policy engines (Kyverno, or OPA/Gatekeeper) and cost-attribution tooling (OpenCost/Kubecost) for per-tenant chargeback/showback.

Experience with managed database platforms or Postgres.

Familiarity with OpenTelemetry, Prometheus/VictoriaMetrics, Grafana, or similar observability tooling.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

#304. XRP Takes Over New York City Subway Advertising

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

XRP's ambitious marketing drive is becoming increasingly evident after the cryptocurrency appeared on subway trains and stations on Manhattan's 42nd Street.

Recent videos have revealed subway cars plastered with the crypto's large white logo, set against a black backdrop.

@XRP will skyrocket soon @Ripple is up to something big. This is in New York City 42nd street train station even inside of the train everything XRP. Like @RosieRios said the train has left the station. @MTA even knows its just like @bgarlinghouse said XRP is the North Star. https://t.co/QkrzClFEzA

The striking marketing campaign also includes American flags and the slogan "Crypto Responsibly." The slogan is a key element of XRP's current marketing strategy.

The marketing effort reportedly extends beyond the subway trains' exterior: XRP's logo and branding are visible within the subway cars and the station itself.

The marketing blitz comes at a crucial time for XRP, as Ripple is set to host its premier Swell conference this week in New York City.

The subway advertising campaign aligns with XRP's push to create a unique brand identity in the public domain.

XRP officially launched its X account on Oct. 19. The account is a collaborative initiative between Ripple and the XRP Ledger Foundation.

In the first tweet, the account simply said: "Hello, world. It's about time."

This launch coincided with the release of a new website, XRP.org, that focuses on the cryptocurrency's use cases such as cross-border payments and low-cost transactions.

The "Crypto Responsibly" slogan is prominently featured on the site, echoing the marketing message seen on NYC's subway trains.

The NYC subway advertising is not XRP's first foray into mainstream marketing this year.

In July, Ripple announced a multi-year partnership with the University of Kansas. The partnership introduced XRP branding to the university's sports teams, including their football and basketball teams.

As yet, there is no official confirmation about the financial outlay, length, or extent of the NYC subway marketing campaign.

The timing of the subway marketing campaign is particularly interesting, given the string of high-profile XRP events in NYC.

The AI & Fintech XRP Ledger Hackathon took place in NYC on Oct. 24–25. This was a 36-hour coding marathon organized by XRPL Commons.

Major industry names like Ripple, Mastercard, Ledger, Amazon Web Services, and QuickNode partnered for the hackathon.

The hackathon was deliberately timed before Ripple's premier Swell conference. Swell is scheduled from Oct. 27 to Oct. 29.
