#307. Big Week Ahead: Ripple Heads to NYC With Wall Street Heavyweights

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

This week, Ripple will bring 1,500 of the world's top decision-makers across traditional finance, crypto, academia and the XRP ecosystem to New York City.

Ripple is preparing for a major week ahead as the Swell 2026 conference brings together heavyweights from Wall Street, policymakers and C-suite leaders from banks, fintechs, corporates and asset managers to discuss the future of finance.

From October 27–29, 2026 at The Shed in Manhattan, leaders from across finance, crypto and the XRP community will come together for talks, panels and discussions.

For the first time in the event's history, Ripple is combining Swell with Apex—its summit for the developers, institutions, and investors building on the XRP Ledger—and UBRI Connect, the academic conference for Ripple's University Blockchain Research Initiative.

Overheard on the streets of the Lower East Side: "Ripple is a vibe" @RippleSwell everywhere!

The speaker lineup includes Ripple CEO Brad Garlinghouse, Ripple President Monica Long, alongside executives from major financial and technology companies including Terrence Duffy of CME Group, Tom Farley, CEO of Bullish, and senior representatives from BNY, Coinbase, Robinhood, Barclays, State Street and Jump Trading. Actor Matt Damon will also participate alongside Gary White, co-founder and CEO of Water.org.

New speakers have been added to the Swell lineup, including Luca Cosentino, Head of Digital Assets at Cross River; R.P. Eddy, CEO of Ergo; Lucas Zandoná Gomes, Community Manager at RedotPay; and Kazım Rıfat Özyılmaz, Co-Founder of Arf.

Swell has historically been where Ripple makes its most significant product and partnership announcements.

Ahead of the big event, Ripple and the XRP ecosystem have received recent announcements. In the past week, Arf announced a partnership with Ripple to bring RLUSD-powered settlement liquidity to licensed cross-border payment companies on the XRP Ledger (XRPL).

Ripple also announced a partnership with Cross River Bank to integrate RLUSD on the XRP Ledger into its core banking platform.

Meritz Securities announced a partnership with Ripple to bring institutional-grade custody and tokenization to Korea's capital markets. This is Ripple's first securities partner in Korea and its fourth Korean institution this year, after Kyobo Life Insurance, Kbank and Jeonbuk Bank.

Ripple is partnering with Privy, a Stripe company, to bring embedded wallets to the XRP Ledger. The integration will also enable agentic commerce through programmable wallets that let AI agents hold funds and transact, including through x402 payments.

#423. Cyberpunk: Edgerunners Season 2 is more than a retread of Netflix's beloved first season - it's a story that truly understands what it means to exist in Night City

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Last night, I sat down with a greasy pizza and a few bottles of beer and braced myself for 10 episodes of heartbreak. Cyberpunk: Edgerunners' second season is out, and it follows in the footsteps of Studio Trigger's beloved, and tragic, debut. My expectations were way off.

Not because it wasn't good: Edgerunners Season 2 is fantastic. Packed with a wonderful cast of troubled characters, their story brought to life with gorgeous and often unorthodox animation. The soundtrack, the voice acting... Quality-wise the bar is as high as the first season. What surprised me was the decision to bring something many may find peculiar given Cyberpunk's dire setting and desperate energy. The show is hopeful. Defiant, even.

In the show's opening episode, we are introduced to characters at rock bottom. King, having suffered from cyberpsychosis, has had his augmentations stripped out of his body, and lost his home. D's in the midst of a lonely journey of revenge, while Talia is rubbing elbows with the Maelstrom gang. Roman, a 12-year-old, is broke and struggling to pursue filmmaking in a world that's left the medium behind. In the pursuit of money, he is nearly diced up.

These characters have all the reason in the world to lose themselves to despair, to burn out. Some almost do. However, as the season progresses, they each find a reason to push on, to live in spite of the odds. By the end of the season, while their problems may not have vanished, those who can choose to push on regardless of what life may throw at them. There are more smiles and laughs in the final episode than there are in the first. In the world of Cyberpunk, that's surprising. Defiant.

Violence in Cyberpunk: Edgerunners Season 2 is not treated as some awesome, cathartic spectacle. Through the literal lens of Roman, it's seen as the monstrous act it is. Characters are urged to not go out and kill people - even those who arguably deserve it - consistently throughout the show. In the first season, we saw the brutal reality of killing in David's descent into cyberpsychosis. In this season, characters without that particular affliction are confronted with the futility of the act.

Around halfway through the season, a cowering lady is half-heartedly executed by a side character, and the show lingers on the scene as her head slumps down onto the ground. It's a grisly moment, a siren blaring starkly, broadcasting a very simple message: violence is not the answer.

There is a sentiment that there are no good endings in Night City, and it's a fair belief to have. Magda Zych, senior writer at CD Projekt Red, described the game's story as a 'noir tragedy', and the dystopian world of the tabletop RPG presents a similarly sad motif. Crime is rampant, people are victimised for no reason, people are chewed up and spat out. This was true in Mike Pondsmith's original vision for Night City, it was true in Cyberpunk 2077, and it's true in Cyberpunk: Edgerunners Season 2.

So there's courage in living as best you can in spite of the injustice of the world. If the first season of Cyberpunk: Edgerunners could be aligned, roughly, to 2077's 'The Sun' ending (where V charges into Arasaka in a blaze of glory), Season 2 can be seen as a parallel to 'The Star' ending, where V takes what time they have left and spends it with their friends and loved ones, divorced from the grinding reality of Night City's grinding concrete. Some NPCs react to this decision during the credits with scorn; it is the antithesis of the 'burn bright, die young' Edgerunner philosophy.

I imagine this season may not have met the expectations of many viewers, who are left cold by how different it is from the first season. And yeah, it would have been entirely acceptable to rehash the same sort of story we saw in 2022 again here, and many people - including myself - would have loved it. But to choose not to do that, to steer the narrative into a totally different direction, is smart. You might even say... defiant.

#359. Families and Students Need Guidance on AI

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Delaware families and policymakers are wrestling with the same issue troubling Americans nationwide: how to handle artificial intelligence.

In 2024, lawmakers in the Blue Hen State created a committee to study AI. Good thing, because parents and teachers badly need guidance on this issue.

Yet AI technology has advanced rapidly over the past two years, so the committee should prioritize drafting recommendations for K-12 schools.

To date, state officials have set only “voluntary” guardrails around AI use in elementary and secondary schools, according to the Delaware-based Caesar Rodney Institute. In May, legislators considered a proposal to create a pilot program that would allow 12 schools to use AI technology.

>>> What’s Really Behind the Call to Regulate AI

Still, the final report on the activities would not be due until 2029, and this year’s freshman class will be seniors by then. These students will be gone before lawmakers act on the report’s findings. Instead, lawmakers should focus on helping students in school today.

Besides, given the pace of change in AI technology, this proposal is too little, too late.

Fortunately, legislative models already exist that lawmakers can apply to allow the use of AI in schools, protect student data and prohibit nonacademic AI activities in classrooms.

Heritage Foundation research explains that lawmakers need not explicitly ban or allow the use of AI in schools. For school officials who allow AI in classrooms, however, educators should adopt training programs for teachers and students. These programs should help teachers and students draft prompts and questions for generative AI bots; create media using AI, such as audio, video and pictures; edit student writing; detect biases in AI results; evaluate different AI bots to decide which are the most effective; and protect student privacy.

Local school boards should adopt policies stating that students may use AI for schoolwork only when specifically allowed by teachers.

The U.S. Department of Education is urging state and local authorities to develop solutions. “Research and experience have demonstrated that technology is a valuable instructional tool when it supports strong pedagogy and effective teaching practices,” writes Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary for elementary and secondary education. “The U.S. Department of Education believes these decisions [about education technology] are best made by states and local communities.”

Policymakers should move quickly. Even if self-reported student usage numbers are slightly inflated, the share of students using AI remains remarkable.

Education officials in large school systems such as New York City and Los Angeles are attempting to prohibit the use of AI for this school year. With so many students already using bots, however, a ban on AI will be difficult to enforce.

>>> Meta Abandons Its AI-Generating Tool, but Public-Safety Guardrails Are Needed

Instead of prohibiting AI, policymakers should draw on existing research to select appropriate educational applications of the technology. For example, lawmakers should block the use of companion bots that simulate personal interactions and develop relationships with users.

Instead, educators can give students access to generative bots that use data to summarize complex information. Teachers can benefit from AI programs that help organize lesson plans. When teachers assign students to use AI, students should report how they used the technology.

Parents should have the choice to opt out of these assignments and have students complete the work using other methods.

State policymakers must focus on boosting student outcomes as technology evolves. Federal officials agree. Ms. Baesler writes: “The question should not be whether teachers and students use technology. The question should be whether the technology we ask teachers and students to use improves learning and students’ academic outcomes.”

As Delaware’s AI committee drafts recommendations, officials should focus on student success. Those on both sides of the political aisle—and on either side of AI debates—can agree on this.

Then, parents and teachers can share best practices and offer solutions to help students across all 50 states.

#55. Paris Saint-Germain sparkle on the pitch while the city of light burns around them | Barney Ronay

📅 Oct 21, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

PSG, pet project of the state of Qatar, expresses the dualism of the city, a place of deprivation where the centre is perfect

Those who sought to bring down the Republic will have to answer for it.

Tell you who would have loved this? Who would have been utterly engrossed scrolling the hashtag #Paris on Tuesday night as two things happened simultaneously, the harrowing of the Barcelona defence at the Parc des Princes, and the harrowing of Paris itself via tales of city-centre thuggery and end-of-days political doom‑mongering.

The big man, Guy Debord himself. The Society of the Spectacle, you say? Actually, I think we’re past that now. Hold the society bit. We’ll just take the spectacle.

The great Parisian Situationist philosopher wasn’t really one for managed fun. But it was impossible not to be bowled over by the extraordinary pre-match show for Paris Saint‑Germain versus Barcelona, Champions League Matchday Three.

The wild pyrotechnics, the plumes of smoke, the full-wrap light show, the lime-green robo‑text spelling out every player’s name on the pitch. Although not the Barcelona names, sadly, for anyone with an unrealised desire to see the words “ANTHONY GORDON” projected in vast letters for the delight of a shrieking crowd.

Admittedly, the Halloween‑themed tifo banner showing a masked Paris Saint-Germain serial killer-type literally butchering the Barcelona badge to death with his bloodied chainsaw was perhaps a bit much. As were the Barça Puta signs, which are now reportedly the subject of a complaint to Uefa.

But it was a wild and all‑consuming spectacle, annihilating every other impulse in your head to a green thought in a green shade. And in the process capturing the profound sense of cognitive dissonance in the city.

Just walking around the streets of central Paris earlier in the day generated its own mild whiplash. On the face of it this was still the same city of flâneurs and tourists and everyday bistro business, and not, repeat not, a civil war drama or some kind of emergent military caliphate.

But the sense of dread was also tangible. You still got the occasional crunch of glass underfoot. Here and there charred bikes and scooters had been politely piled to one side. Even close to the Parc, away from the main hotspots, shopkeepers could be seen emerging warily into their doorways at the sound of a police siren.

By Tuesday night, a total of 2,513 people had been taken into police custody after the events of the weekend. At least 420 rental bikes had been destroyed or maimed. Liberated high-value retail goods were already being sold on Vinted, a commendable show of entrepreneurial energy from the maligned younger generation.

Not that you’d suspect these two worlds had any kind of overlap, lost in the booming triumphalism around PSG’s 4–2 win, one of the most beautifully luminous football matches you’re ever likely to see. Or indeed reading the coverage of an event that seems to have been treated in France like its own clear, clean space, floating above the flames, all the chat about the brilliance of Monsieur Dynamite, Désiré Doué, the wonderful television audiences on Canal+ TV, the whole of European football “kneeling before the machine” (are we sure this isn’t to do with anarchic rejection of social control?). “Paris est insubmersible!” was the headline in Le Parisien. But is it?

Remember when football used to reflect the world? Or just provide clues, a commentary, some kind of metaphor? This is no longer the case. At a time when even the most thunderous of commentators is struggling to summarise all this in suitably trite and damning terms, it was hard to avoid the feeling Paris Saint-Germain, the pet power football project of the state of Qatar, expresses this dualism perfectly.

Sometimes you just have to let football tell you things. “People who want to cause trouble in the city centres next Saturday will be prevented,” Emmanuel Macron announced on Tuesday evening, speaking, as it happens, from Doha, where he met Sheikh Tamim bin Hamad al-Thani, ultimate owner of PSG.

Macron is said to be considering deploying the French military to keep the peace on the streets before the next planned protests, although even here he may have to ask Qatar’s permission if he wants to deploy any of the French troops currently deployed working its missile systems, or embedded in the Doha security services.

The key event Macron is tooling up for is this Saturday night, 24 October, the date the gilets jaunes social media feed has set for an even greater swell of disorder. And again this will be football adjacent, with PSG playing Lyon at home on Sunday, a top‑of‑the-table capital‑city clash played out against whatever wreckage is left, however many thousands of Dot bikes are savaged, whatever guerrilla skirmishes are still playing out in the Marais.

What does this really have to do with football and the Ici C’est Paris brand appropriated by a state outreach project? Well, what are these demonstrations anyway? There have been many theories put forward as to what exactly is happening here, what is the spark, the kindling, the fire behind this disorder. School protests were the original line. Others have now roped in some kind of nebulous omni-cause, Instagram-activism, perhaps even a barbarian takeover, the decline of the west, savagery at the gates, of the type Donald Trump pretends to see.

Do you need a single reason? Take a look around. Modern life is pretty alarming on a minute‑to‑minute basis. Disaffection, alienation, a sense of being transformed in real time into a passive economic consumer unit, a set of eyeballs, an avatar of consumption. French youth unemployment is close to 20%. Robots nobody wants are being lined up to take jobs that don’t exist. Why don’t these young people want to join a youth club? Why don’t they sign up for the Liberal Democrats? Why are they enjoying empowered disorder? Maybe because nothing is yours around here, not even the things thrust into your brain as digital distractions. So why not grab something when you can? Debord would have called this “a vicious circle of isolation”, lonely crowd, technologies based on isolation.

On the other hand, what is PSG in Paris, other than the perfect expression of this? It often seems to surprise people that celebrations of PSG victories end up with riots and burnt-out cars. But Paris is both a beautiful and deeply dysfunctional city, a place of make‑believe and deprivation, where the centre is a perfect, performative relic, the suburbs shunted out to the ring roads, a life lived inside a box, noses pressed up against the glass, peering through your binoculars at the city of someone else’s love.

Paris is the divide between overclass and churn expressed in a single city. It’s everything you might have had but never will. And PSG represent this like no other entity, a football club owned as an act of outreach by a staggeringly rich inherited monarchy, weaponised passion, borrowed culture, engineered spectacle, deeply cynical but also deeply seductive.

Why are the Qataris splurging hundreds of millions into this project? Because they just really like high-pressing positional football? Qatar is the indentured labour state that employs Colin Kaepernick to promote its outreach projects. It will hymn its connections to the footballers of the banlieue, which is true and real and valuable, but not because of anything this political project wants or needs other than public relations.

It is in its own way a perfect human lab experiment, synthesised beauty, collectivism, with a cold, cold hand at its heart. And this is why there weren’t two versions of Paris in evidence on Tuesday night, just the same one, the dark side and the shiny side, an engine of the same divisions, the same kind of violence, right down to the sight of kids in PSG training gear dodging the police or taunting the barricades at the Place de la République.

And now we have Sunday night to come, more Ici C’est Paris, more PASSION. What kind of background will it be played out against? Does it matter under the lights? Will anyone notice?

Le Monde has described these events as a very basic test of the republic, its ability to bend, to remain essentially itself, the state’s willingness to pit itself against its own people. Football, as ever, holds these answers already. PSG’s Qatari owners have spent the past 15 years flaunting this city like a global luxury brand, extending its reach, creating its own ideal of conspicuous consumption, spectacle, triumph, son et lumière, bread and roses. As ever, if you want to know how and why, football has the answers.

#362. Iran, China, and Cuba Are Directing Protests Inside America

📅 Oct 21, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Evidence is growing that the tyrannical regimes of China, Iran, and Cuba are not only financing protests in American streets, but actively directing them.

The Network Contagion Research Institute, whose work in these areas has been indispensable for years, recently published another groundbreaking study that blows the lid off how this foreign network operates.

The report indicates that an Iranian influence node that takes orders from the Islamic Republic regime, the House of Latin America, conducts what NCRI calls “high-impact domestic U.S. influence operations.”

“For nearly two decades,” NCRI says, HOLA “has functioned as a primary bridge between the Iranian regime, Latin American socialist governments, and U.S. anti-war coalitions, with its American-based operatives acting as undeclared agents of a foreign government on U.S. soil.”

It gets much worse. HOLA, it says, “has now formally merged with Neville Roy Singham’s Chinese Communist Party-linked Tricontinental network to form a unified Iran-China operation targeting the United States from the south.”

>>> Trojan Horses: Covert Foreign Malign Influence Operations in America

Neville Roy Singham is an American who lives in Shanghai, has close ties to the Chinese Communist Party, and uses the nearly $800 million he made in the 2017 sale of a tech consulting firm to fund communist causes worldwide.

All of this means that “Iran and China have now merged their U.S. influence operations into a single machine,” the report says.

This means that many of the protests in America aren’t organic, but actually Kabuki street theater perpetrated by a red-green alliance of communist and Islamist groups. Less well known is the involvement of malign foreign interests.

Hillary Clinton recently got into trouble online not for celebrating street mayhem, but for saying that what was wrong was not the protests, but white Christians trying to stop societal and cultural decay.

“People have been protesting, you know, for hundreds of years, that, you know, things were not as they should be, given our ideals and how we should be moving toward them. So I think that’s what makes us so special as a country,” she said on the liberal confessional stage offered by MS Now’s Morning Joe every weekday morning.

“The idea that you could turn the clock back and try to recreate a world that never was, dominated by, you know, let’s say it, white men, of a certain persuasion, certain religion, a certain point of view, a certain ideology, is just doing such damage to what we should be aiming for,” she averred.

I was cheered, though, to hear Hillary confirm that conservatives have derailed the liberal project, at least temporarily: “We were on the path toward that … we were on the right trajectory.”

NCRI has consistently documented the nefarious foreign involvement in the protests that are trying to undo this conservative restoration; its new report is but the latest. In 2024, it was one of the first research outfits to spotlight Singham and his network, a report that led to a slew of media reports, congressional hearings, and a federal investigation.

Later that year, and again in 2025, NCRI shone its bright light on the Middle Eastern Children’s Alliance (which sports yet another catchy acronym, MECA). Its 2025 investigation, it says, yielded evidence suggesting that MECA had “fiscal and personnel ties to entities associated with the Popular Front for the Liberation of Palestine (PFLP),” which the U.S. government has designated as a foreign terrorist organization.

In addition to these unsavory connections, and others in the Gaza Strip, MECA has recently “begun facilitating fundraising for a network of extremist anti-government actors based in the United States.”

MECA, for example, is listed as a partner in a fundraising campaign initiated by the Singham-linked Shut It Down for Palestine Marxist-Islamist coalition of groups that lead urban marches in America. The Singham-funded People’s Forum, one of the Marxist outfits in the SID4P, also reported in 2024 a grant of $16,420 to MECA, described as a “Humanitarian Relief Fundraiser.”

MECA is also involved in the effort to indoctrinate our children by penetrating our K-12 public school system. MECA created the Teach Palestine radical curriculum which, according to the 2025 NCRI report, enjoys “widespread circulation” because it was adopted by Rethinking Schools and the Zinn Education Project, two radical curriculum and teaching material producers.

MECA, however, enjoys support from what could be called the far-left establishment. Its Alliance of Founding Advisors has included the writer Maya Angelou, the Marxist academic Noam Chomsky, and Rep. Maxine Waters (D-CA). It receives funding from organizations inside billionaire George Soros’s Open Society Foundations, the Rockefeller Brothers Fund, and the NoVo Foundation.

But the merging of China, Iran, and Cuba into a single machine influence operation takes things to a different level. The key link here is between HOLA and Tricontinental’s boss, Vijay Prashad, an Indian communist academic with close ties to Cuba’s regime, whose Politburo he met with this year, and a close friend of Singham’s.

One of Prashad’s contacts in HOLA is Ali Abutalebi, the former digital communications director for former President Mahmoud Ahmadinejad, whose two terms in office in 2005-13 were one of the most radical of the Islamic Republic.

According to NCRI’s new report, “Abutalebi manages Tricontinental’s Persian-language digital infrastructure.” The two organizations also “co-publish content and jointly raise funds for Cuban solidarity campaigns routing donations through OFAC [the Treasury Department’s Office of Foreign Assets Control]-designated Iranian banks—including one designated specifically for financing Iran’s Ministry of Defense weapons procurement.”

In other words, Ahmadinejad’s digital guy now manages digital infrastructure for an American-registered nonprofit organization with close ties to the CCP and which does Cuba’s dirty work here.

>>> “We Have To Smash the State”: The Left Prepares for War

Worse, Abutalebi is now domiciled in Chicago—“the Iranian regime’s asset on the ground” in the Windy City, as the NCRI puts it. “He organizes protests alongside U.S. socialist and anti-government coalitions, coordinates Iranian regime national days of action on American streets, and raises funds in OFAC-sanctioned Iranian banks—all without a FARA registration.”

Another key figure in the growing relationship between HOLA and the Chinese-influenced Singham network is HOLA founder Abdolhamid Shahrabi, which the NCRI describes as “one of the Iranian regime’s most important influence operators in Latin America.”

It was Shahrabi who organized Cuban dictator Fidel Castro’s visit to Tehran in 2003. Twenty years later, Shahrabi organized a conference at the University of Tehran attended by the ambassadors of Cuba, Venezuela, Bolivia, and Nicaragua, and Tricontinental’s Prashad.

The NCRI, finally, cites hacked internal records from Press TV, which is Iran’s OFAC-sanctioned English-language state propaganda outlet, which reveal that Press TV contacted by telephone key American partners of Shahrabi ahead of U.S. protests.

“The United National Antiwar Coalition and Workers World Party received no fewer than 260 combined contacts over a 29-month period from 2019 to 2022,” the report said. “Call volume spikes consistently preceded U.S.-based protest surges by roughly one month, indicating that Iranian state media engagement.”

If that doesn’t get our leaders’ attention, not much will.

#292. Stranger Than Heaven Is One Man's Story Of Biculturalism, Music, Fitting In, And Fighting, Told Over 50 Years

📅 Oct 21, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Stranger Than Heaven is one of the early 2027 releases we're most looking forward to, thanks in large part to it being an ambitious title that tells a prequel story following the same man over the course of 50 years. Players control Makoto Daito, a half-American half-Japanese man stuck between two cultures, struggling to fully fit in with either. In 1915, he stows away on a San Francisco ship bound for Japan in an attempt to escape the prejudice he experienced in the United States as an Asian man. However, once he arrives in Japan, he doesn't quite find the culture of acceptance he expected. As he wanders the streets, pulling off jobs for Orpheus (portrayed by rapper Snoop Dogg), I hear the folks Kokura, where Makoto landed in Japan, jeering him behind his back.

It's only one local, Tae Matsumoto, who seems to see Makoto for who he is. Makoto goes to the bar where Tae works, in an attempt to undermine a rival gang, but once he spends an evening with her, he confesses the entire thing before asking Tae out on a real date. Unfortunately for Makoto, Tae is the mistress of the Oki Clan, and when Oki finds out that Makoto is spending time with his mistress, he puts a hit on Makoto and steals Tae away. Makoto leads a siege against the ironworks mill where Oki has stolen Tae, resulting in the most climactic clash of my three hours with Stranger Than Heaven: a battle against the hulking Shimura. There, I really put my mastery of the unique combat system, which corresponds the shoulder buttons to your arms and the triggers to your legs for a more direct control of the combos you throw. Since Shimura uses a huge melee weapon in his second phase, I also used my weapon of choice: a knife. Using the nimbleness afforded by a lightweight weapon, I dodged his advances and finished him off to save Tae.

But Kokura in 1915 is hardly the only setting of the game, as players will follow Makoto's exploits across five decades, visiting Kure in 1919, Osaka in 1943, Atami in 1951, and, finally, Shinjuku in 1965. I had a chance to free-roam explore both Kure and Osaka. I experienced the fighting arena of Kure, where you challenge increasingly difficult opponents (and can re-challenge defeated foes with special win conditions), and stumbled upon a masked serial killer who gave me the toughest fight of my life. I also found a composer, who can help craft songs based on sounds from which Makoto pulls inspiration; during my playthrough, Makoto found inspiration in everything from a crying baby in a house he walked by to the clang of his metallic melee weapon cracking against a thug's skull.

In the Osaka sequence, the attitude toward Makoto has shifted dramatically; men and women alike run up to and greet him with bright smiles due to his work in organized crime and music showbiz. To that end, I use this gameplay section to live in that world. After negotiating with local business owners to buy ad space on their billboards, I have Makoto put on a show at a local theater. Here, I can choose the program, performers, and then take part in an interactive performance.

I left my hands-on session extremely impressed by the many layers present in Stranger Than Heaven. Following my play sessions, I sat down with Stranger Than Heaven director Mikinobu Abe and lead actor Yu Shirota to discuss the themes and inspiration behind RGG Studio's new entry in the Yakuza/Like a Dragon universe.

When you're getting to the point where you're conceptualizing your next game, what are the conversations like that led you to creating a prequel title to the Yakuza/Like a Dragon series with a unique premise like we see in Stranger Than Heaven?Mikinobu Abe, Director: In the beginning, there wasn't about, "Okay, let's make this game as something that's gonna connect to the Yakuza or Like a Dragon franchise." It was something more of we always wondered while making Yakuza and Like a Dragon games how would someone create such an organization, like a yakuza organization, and that could be an interesting narrative to focus on. In the Yakuza franchise, we have the Tojo Clan or the Omi Alliance, but we never actually told why or who would create such a thing. So, the starting point was from there, and then, as we were developing the narrative, we added all sorts of elements.

One of the greatest ideas for the game is making the main character of mixed heritage of Japanese and American. I think that decision really opened up a whole new narrative to tell for the studio. So, from there, we did want to tell the beginning of such an organization, but it wasn't like we wanted to tell a prologue of the Yakuza or Like a Dragon series. We already had the mind of, like, "Okay, we want to make this as something brand new and like nothing that we have made before."

And with the theme of biculturalism relating to Makoto Daito, why was telling the story of existing in that "in between" space where maybe you don't feel like you belong in either culture such an important story to tell with this game?MA: At the beginning, it wasn't a story about mixed heritage; it was just one Japanese guy who's trying to create an organization. But we wanted to make sure that the character has a strong motivation for creating such an organization, and we felt like it would be a great narrative to tell if we made the main character as a mixed heritage of both Japanese and American. That really gave us the creative freedom to add an American character and do a multilingual setting where American characters speak in English and Japanese characters speak in Japanese, which is such an interesting thing. And I don't think I've ever seen that in a video game, so it really opened up a lot of creative ideas from there. We felt doing that theme really strong makes the narrative stronger in the way that we wanted to depict this whole story and gameplay experience.

Yu Shirota, Actor Portraying Makoto Daito: For Makoto Daito and myself, there are struggles that come from being in between two cultures or two countries, and the story does see how he grows through those struggles. Just as a disclaimer: These are my personal thoughts, and not necessarily Sega's thoughts or [Abe]'s thoughts. But, in the current world, not just with race, but many people struggle with finding their identity and who they are. For Daito, the main struggle is, again, two countries, and it's a story of finding himself through that. But the people who may play this game, when you go around, you might get labeled, you might get told things, and for those people that are really struggling with that, Makoto Daito's story is something that can give them the courage to find their true selves. I think that Makoto Daito's identity of the two countries plays an important role in doing that.

In the early stages of the game, we see Makoto leave the United States for Japan, hoping he'll be more accepted, but then, when he arrives, he faces more prejudice. Can you talk about what Makoto is going through at that point, and how his attitude towards his new home, Japan, evolves over the course of those early years?YS: I have definitely gone through the same thing he does at first. When I was younger, I felt like an outsider in Spain, and then I came to Japan, thinking I'm coming back home, but I faced sort of the same thing as I was facing in Spain. This is exactly the same as what Daito goes through when he comes from America to Japan, so it was very easy to put myself in his shoes. It didn't really take a lot to imagine how he's feeling, but in terms of being in between, I do think these struggles are something that only people like us can truly understand.

In the beginning of the story, after Daito comes to Japan, he gets called "ketō," which is sort of a slur for Westerners in that age in Japan, and he doesn't know what that word means. So, he's like, "What are they calling me?" He starts off talking to people around town, but his Japanese is not proficient yet, and so they just brush him off as a foreigner. And, of course, seeing this myself, it feels sad, but also, it feels very nostalgic, too, because I know exactly how that feels.

MA: When it comes to gameplay, when we were doing motion capture and the voice acting in general – not just Makoto, but other characters who join the journey throughout the eras – one of the things we really focused on was how Japanese Daito is at the time. When you start the game, Daito's body language and his first language is in more English. The way the character has this personal space between the person and the way he moves and everything is more Westernized. Once you progress the game, you start to notice that Daito is becoming a little bit more Japanese in a way that he knows how to communicate like a Japanese person, or even someone who's really higher up.

We pay close attention on how the transition from Makoto living in Japan and the way he would act in both motion acting and facial animation, as well. Like, how fluent he is in Japanese would really affect the lip animations, so we really put a focus on those and crafted this character. This experience might feel like it's really just for Makoto, but I truly believe that we all have something close in our life that we can resonate with Makoto, like going to a new school or working in a new workplace. You're always going to start as a stranger. It might not be as harsh as Makoto['s experience], but I really believe that this story and that attention to detail would resonate with people across the globe.

So, as we play through the eras, we'll be able to notice Makoto's improved Japanese as he lives in the country for longer?MA: I have to give huge credit to [Shirota]. He did an amazing job! Getting those small, small details! The bigger ones, for example, with the battle voices, when you beat someone up, initially, like a "Take this!" or "You bastard!" Then, once time progresses, it slowly becomes Japanese.

YS: It was really difficult, especially the age and the thoughts. When he came to Japan, he was thinking in English, so the thoughts or things he said to himself were in English. But in 5 or 10 years, it changed to Japanese, and that was easier for me, of course, because English is my third language, so the balance was really interesting for me, and of course I had the same thing in my life: Spanish to Japanese. That was really fun for me, and then of course [progressing Makoto's] age. That was really interesting! The teenager Makoto and '60s Makoto are totally different. You can tell with the voice.

MA: It's also worth mentioning that within Japan, we do have a lot of accents, and because we go to Hiroshima and Osaka, that alone has a different Japanese accent, and it's a huge difference. And he nailed all the Japanese accents as well. I can't wait for people to see this amazing work.

But it does seem like one you were particularly well-suited for, given your personal experience.YS: Yes, I think my experience that I've had in my life – every single experience – I could put that into Makoto Daito, and that was really helpful to create this character. I'm really happy to play this role.

When we see Daito first arrive in Kokura in 1915, it seemed like he's really not trying to stick out. He's just trying to fit in as best he can, and he's really worried about being a foreigner. Then, in the next era, he has bleached hair, and he seems like maybe he's not so concerned about sticking out, and maybe leaning into the fact that he is different.MA: I would say that's a great read, but however, I'm trying not to spoil anything, but the blonde Daito... there is a cutscene that we revealed during Tokyo Game Show where Daito and a character called Hama is speaking, and a few hints might be in that cutscene. I can't tell you any further than this, so let me expand the question just a little bit. Daito is going to start finding his own identity throughout life and throughout the eras, and the character visualization alone really represents what Daito is going through within each city in those eras. We have a lot of small hints here and there, so some of them might be too small that even the game releases and no one notices! [laughs]

But, we really tried our best to keep those details in the game. I truly hope that people pick up on those small details about how much he fits into this Japanese country. There might be situations where he chooses not to fit in, as well, so I hope people will pick up on those small details.

One of the big selling points for Stranger Than Heaven is that we get to know Makoto Daito over a 50-year span of his life. What challenges did that bring with getting that level of familiarity with a character?MA: You're going to get to know everything about him! [laughs] You're gonna witness his whole life! It was a really hard challenge depicting a man's life, because there are certain characters – and not just Makoto, but his friend Yu Shinjo, who's played by Dean Fujioka. It was really hard because there are certain characters that we don't even know how they're gonna age as an actual actor, because Dean-san's in his 40s, and when we were doing the character modeling, we had to make, like, an older version of him, and we have no idea how he will look, so those are some things that were really hard about developing the game.

For a character like Bunta Sugawara's Iwaki, we have all the reference pictures throughout his ages, so I wouldn't say it was easy – the character team will kill me if I say it was easy! [laughs] – but we had the references. Aging a character was a huge challenge for us tackling this project. For me personally, I'm still in my 20s; I'm 29! So, from Makoto Daito's life, there's this certain point where I have no idea how he would feel at that age and time.

YS: I personally only have 40 years on this Earth so far, so the experiences and challenges that someone might face within those 40 years, I could imagine more easily because I have the experience with that. But the last era in Shinjuku, Daito goes over this age, so I can only try to imagine what it would be like. That was a really fun experience, and I can't really talk about it right now due to spoilers, but the challenges that shape Daito over the course of the 50 years to make him the Shinjuku Daito that he ends up as, thinking about that was very interesting.

I noticed over the course of different eras, people react differently to Makoto. They go from calling him names and telling him to get out of Kokura to running up to you while you're walking around Osaka and complimenting you. It seems like a complete 180 from when he arrived in 1915. How does that reaction evolve and how does that impact him as he carries himself through the ages?MA: He will go through a lot of identities throughout his life. When he gets to Japan, he's a no one. Then, he's going to join the Yashima Trading Company, which is led by Yashima, and from there, he's treated as a member of the Yashima Company. Then, once he's in Kure, he's gonna join the Iwaki Clan, which is a big part of the narrative, as well. And in Osaka, he's going to start a promoting company with Yu Shinjo. I feel like those changes of identity really affects how people react to his title, and we carefully created how that affects Makoto as gameplay.

But not just gameplay, but with all the cutscenes, we try to make sure the player has the sense of how the people are looking at Makoto. So, when we have all the people walking by, you can tell Makoto is seen as someone who's unwanted there, or maybe in another scene, you can see that no one's really given too much care to him, so all the background acting for the cutscenes was really crafted based on how Makoto's identities are at the time. I really feel like the player can experience the environment of Makoto through both the gameplay and the narrative.

YS: Again, I see that it overlaps with my personal experiences. Daito, at first, he's very young. He's scared. He doesn't know what to do exactly, but he's sort of forced to assimilate. Then, as time goes on, he finds his worth and grows as a person, and with that, how people look at him starts changing, too. He starts off softer, more easily affected, but as he grows older, he grows a harder shell. He goes into the world of the yakuza. He becomes a member of a clan, and the process of him becoming this harder-shell person is depicted very well throughout the narrative. And the final product, after all of that process of him being called "Boss" by everyone, I also have some similar experiences, because I'm an actor and sort of a celebrity. People come around me and say good things about me, so imagining how it feels as Daito to be in that position was something that I was really able to do without a lot of issues.

As you progress through the story, each era takes place in a different Japanese city. Why were these specific eras tied to these particular cities?MA: Those cities were chosen based purely on the narrative that we wanted to depict. Again, the beginning was that we want to depict why someone would create such an organization, and from there, we started to realize that we'll need a lot of time to tell this narrative. In our previous games, like Yakuza or Like a Dragon, the narrative only revolves around a week, maybe, or somewhere around there. But you wouldn't be able to tell a narrative of someone creating such a huge organization within a week or a day. Then, we settled with, "Okay, it would be cool if we could depict a man's life journey on creating this place that he wants to call home." That was the starting point of how we decided we want to depict a man's life, and we already had in mind that we could start the narrative from 1915 Kokura, because back in the day, Kokura was a huge industrial city, and it was really natural for someone to end up in Kokura [after] smuggling onto a ship.

The starting point of the narrative was already fixed and, for spoiler reasons, I can't talk too much, but the ending point was 1965 Shinjuku from the very beginning. From there, we had two dots, and now we selected the era and the city based off of how interesting it would be to put Makoto in a situation that those eras and cities could offer. For example, 1929 Kure; Kure is a famous city within Japan for being the city that was used in the famous yakuza movie called Battle Without Honor and Humanity, and it was a great fit for if we wanted to tell a story about the yakuza. We thought it would be really interesting if we could put Makoto in that situation in Kure because, again, he has this identity crisis of whether he wants to be Japanese or American. Yakuza is such a Japanese organization, so we thought it would be really interesting to put Makoto in that city. Also, because it's 1929 Kure, back in the day, there were a lot of foreign people within the city because it was such a big military port. That already makes it have this build up of telling a great narrative of someone who thinks they're Japanese and they're in the yakuza organization, and yet he still has this foreigner side of himself, and he has a lot of foreigners within the city to connect with.

How much work did it add to the team's plates to depict not only different cities, but different eras?MA: You can't imagine how hard it was! [laughs] It was hard! We did five cities in the past with Yakuza 5, but this time, because we weren't able to reuse any assets between the cities, and also because no one knows what it was like back in 1915... We had to do a lot of research, and there was some stuff that I, as a Japanese, didn't even know existed! There were times when we were just running around old bookshops and looking for a magazine that was popular in those days. We did a lot of research based off of those, but the goal was never to recreate Japanese history, so we didn't have this historical accuracy concept from the very beginning. So, we took a lot of inspiration from what we have researched, and we wanted to convert that into what we wanted to depict as a game and [gamify] it, so it's in this range of entertaining people. That research took a lot of time, and we did go to all the cities and looked up really old photos and took that research, and had this creative freedom on changing it into something that we wanted to depict in the game.

As you go through these eras between 1915 and 1965, you're landing in some eras that were not only quite dramatically different from one another, but some were also quite tumultuous for Japan. How do you go about addressing those global and national events within the story?MA: So, we do touch some of those historical events that are happened. For example, 1943 Osaka. The setting itself is during wartimes. However, the narrative really leans toward Makoto Daito's life, and so there's not this crucial depiction of those historical events, and that gave us a lot of creative freedom to focus on Daito's life. The events are happening, right? We can't ignore that. But the big events – for example, having a war – it does affect the live of who is living in that city, but they have their own lives, and I think it's worth telling that life more than what history was actually like. And so, the main goal was the really focus on Makoto Daito's life in that situation.

Music plays a huge role in Stranger Than Heaven, but what other pieces of art inspired you and your team that pushed the direction of the game?MA: I studied a lot of film, and I had a lot of interest in movies. Growing up, I always wanted to make movies, and I even went to film school. The knowledge I acquired there was, I did watch a lot of classic movies, and I also studied a lot of film media and camera lenses. And because I had this background, I took a lot of inspiration from how it was created back then. We did a lot of research on these vintage lenses that were used back then, or this film media that was used back then. It wasn't particularly this movie, but it was more the technique they used back then,  for something that was really hard. Let's say for example, putting text on a video; these days, where you just put it in the editing software, but back in the day, they had this, like, glass, and then you wrote on it, and then you had to print it on a film. That alone – that analog technique – really was something that we could make a use of and create this vibe that we've never seen in a computer-generated graphic image.

Our amazing art director, [Daishi] Kanaya-san, has a strong background in oil painting, as well. He did a lot of research on Japanese painting from those eras, and we noticed how the use of lighting was really different from the drawings that would be drawn these days. This aspect of painting from Kanaya-san and the aspect of film from me connected and we created something I personally have never seen before, and Kanaya-san has never seen before. I hope the player finds it intriguing!

That said, I personally love classic Hollywood and gangster movies. I took a lot of inspiration from them, as well as ninkyo movies, which is a Japanese term for a yakuza-themed entertainment, and of course, Bunta Sugawara, who is in our game, is a legendary actor from back in those eras, so we took some inspiration from there, as well.

Did your expertise in film influence how you implemented a camera where you need to wind it before you snap, then take it to the pawn shop to get it developed?MA: Yeah, I really wanted that film camera in there! [laughs] It really is hard, isn't it? I think those small details of using a gadget, especially with my generation in general, we never got to use those analog cameras. I already had a smartphone when I was in junior high school, so I had this dream of, "Oh I wish I could use those analog gadgets back in the days." So, we wanted to make sure that the game has that analog feeling to it.

Also, speaking of the camera, a little bit of a different angle is that I directed most of the cutscenes in the game, and when we were doing the motion capture as well as the camera-angle structuring, we wanted to make the audience feel like they are watching something that would have been recreate-able back in those days, and yet new. The art term was "vintage," so it's not old. It does feel old, but it doesn't feel old in a way that's not cool, in a sense, these days. We really wanted to make sure we nail that sense of vintage, so we carefully crafted, like, "Okay, we don't want to make the camera movement too flashy. Let's stick to how it would have been in the past. Make the lighting really modern, just so it doesn't feel old, but something that does feel vintage." We did a lot of experimenting on that, as well, and I hope the players have this sense of they're watching something vintage, but not something they have seen in a video game before.

Speaking of vintage and retro things, the Yakuza and Like a Dragon franchise have been around for a long time now, but still going strong. How do you pull from that series and its long lineage in a way that still lets Stranger Than Heaven feel like something new while still making sure it feels like a part of the broader universe and story?MA: Stranger Than Heaven's dev team is led by a lot of the younger generation that the studio has, including myself. There was never a conversation of, "Hey, Yakuza looked like this, so let's make this like this." Instead, we were just so focused on creating a game called Stranger Than Heaven. Period. It wasn't like it never came across our mind – this is an RGG game that we created. In a sense, we never actually thought of the Yakuza series while developing this game in a good way, and I think that already makes it so a game can feel new.

Especially with the combat, every single game mechanic that we have in the game usually revolves around the narrative that we want to tell. For example, Kazuma Kiryu, he's a legendary fighter, so we want to make sure the player feels they're strong. That's why we chose that brawling action type of game. And for Ichiban, because his character and narrative revolves around friendship, that's why we chose the turn-based game genre. So, when we were depicting a new character, Makoto Daito, the narrative is going to change.

We knew that we have to create something new for this character, and one of the biggest things is that Makoto starts off not knowing how to hit people. We wanted to make sure that is represented in the control scheme, as well as the experience as a whole. One of the biggest things was that we wanted to create the experience to be really intuitive and have this synchronization-ness between the player and Makoto. We wanted to make sure that they can immerse themselves in Makoto's life, and I think combat immersion is something really special that would represent this gameplay as Makoto. Progressing through the eras, of course, Makoto is going to change, and I hope the player's skills change in a way. Of course, you're gonna get better at the game, and that immersion and synchronization alone, I think, is something really special with this game.

The combat is unique, because it takes you a moment to wrap your brain around it, but once you do, a lightbulb kind of goes off in your head and it becomes pretty intuitive. How did the team land on this combat system?MA: The basic idea was we wanted to make combat intuitive, and we had this one theme of actually going back to the roots of what made yakuza-themed content special in those ninkyo movies. We were looking at a lot of them, and one thing we noticed is how raw the action is. No one's a master at anything, and they're just pulling clothes to fight, and it's not just punching and kicking people; we noticed that if you're not exactly familiar with fighting, punching doesn't happen too much. That rawness alone, we felt that was something really special, something that lies in the ninkyo movies. We took a lot of inspiration from that, and we wanted to make sure Makoto feels like a raw person who's not exactly good at fighting, and we wanted to make sure the player feels that. Going back to the roots of ninkyo movies, the huge concept is that it's either kill or get killed, and we wanted to make sure that the player feels that tension while in a fight. Again, that synchronization that we needed to tell the player that, 'Hey you're in a situation of it's either you kill or your die situation," and that immersion was the core concept of how we wanted to land the combat.

Something else I noticed while exploring was the abundance of minigames. How did you go about making sure that you were not only including era-appropriate games, but also ones that translated well to a video game?MA: Usually within an RGG Studio title, we choose those minigames purely on how we want to depict the city. So, in the Yakuza franchise, it's mostly modern cities, and we have a lot of variations on minigames, such as karaoke or pachinko. They're there to depict the city's vibe as gameplay, and it was no different for us. The minigames within this game represent the cities' and eras' vibes and people's lives back then. Again, we did a lot of research on what was popular back then, and we chose minigames to represent the city's atmosphere. Also, because the eras progress, the minigames will also change. Some of them will be playable the entire game, and some of them are going to only be in one city and one era. And as technology evolves, of course, a lot of other games are going to come in. A lot of them we haven't announced yet, so I hope people enjoy the minigames and get a sense of the atmosphere that the city has to offer.

And we've talked a lot about Daito, but some other characters garnered a lot of attention back during Summer Game Fest's reveal. Why was Snoop Dogg the right choice for the role of Orpheus, and did his casting lead to the casting of Tupac?MA: Every single casting that we made, it was always narrative first for us. When we started writing the narrative, we had this character called Orpheus, who is going to be someone who teaches the ropes to Daito on basically everything. Orpheus is going to teach him how to survive in Japan and how to make money off of music and show business, but Orpheus needs to be a smuggler, who's going to carry [Daito] from America to Japan. He basically needed to be a badass, and also, he has to act because he has a lot of emotional scenes during the game. That already makes it a really hard character to play, and we always had this casting idea of, "It would be so cool if Snoop Dogg would play this role," and we kind of heard that Snoop Dogg and his son had played the previous Yakuza games before, so we reached out and we explained every single story and the character that we wanted to depict using Snoop's likeness, and we created the character together, building up how he should look or how he should talk. It was a great experience creating Orpheus, and that goes for other characters as well.

It's basically the same with not just Tupac, but Bunta Sugawara, who's already passed away. We talked with every single individual who was important to their lives – the estate, the families as well – and we explained everything about the story and why we want them to be in the game. They loved the idea of having them in the game, and we crafted those characters as carefully as possible with the highest respect of not breaking how they would have played if they were alive, in a sense.

For more on the inclusion of Snoop Dogg and Tupac in the cast, read our interview with RGG Studio head Masayoshi Yokoyama here. Stranger Than Heaven arrives on PlayStation 5, Xbox Series X/S, and PC on January 15.

Note: This conversation took part across two back-to-back interviews: one with Mikinobu Abe and one with both Abe and Yu Shirota together. For the purposes of this article, the questions and answers were combined into a singular conversation.

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

Creator and UGC content is only getting bigger as a growth channel for developer tools, and right now Supabase reaches it mostly at arm's length, through agencies that own the actual relationships with creators. The work is currently split on an interim basis across a couple of people on the marketing team; this role is being created to own it end to end.

#296. The demo for city-flattening fighting game Godzilla: Destroy All Monsters Melee Remastered is a roaring good time, no nostalgia required

📅 Oct 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Despite being a lover of the King of the Monsters, I was unfortunately too young to experience Pipeworks Software's Godzilla trilogy of 3D kaiju-fighters back its PS2/Gamecube-era heyday (there was a PS2 in the house as a young child, but I also had no agency to ask for games or money to buy them). As such, I'm not necessarily the intended audience for Godzilla: Destroy All Monsters Melee Remastered, a remake of Pipeworks' first entry back in 2002, but I can gladly tell you thanks to my time with the Steam Next Fest demo that nostalgia is not needed to enjoy this classic made anew.

For readers who were introduced to the Godzilla series via more recent entries such as the Hollywood Monsterverse, 2016's Shin Godzilla or 2023's Godzilla Minus One, DAMM (yes, that's the acronym I'm using) might seem more than a little silly at first. The monsters look more like people in rubber suits than titans that once ruled the earth, and they fly around like ragdolls into the stratosphere when hit particularly hard. This is all very intentional, and beautifully recreates the charm of the Showa (1954-1975) era of Godzilla films, in which the series went from utterly harrowing debut Gojira to the game's namesake, an event movie in which an absolute fuckton of monsters had it out like action figures being slammed together. I really can't overstate how well this game captures the vibe of the Showa era's blend of silliness and seriousness.

As for the 1v1 monstering experience of the remake itself, it's largely unchanged from the original game. The demo offers a singleplayer 'Destruction' mode which lets you pick a monster of your choosing to absolutely wreck an unsuspecting city — the demo would only let me choose Seattle as a stage, which I found kind of hilarious; that poor Space Needle — and gain a high score for causing as much mayhem as possible. The star of the show, though, is Versus, an arena mode that can be played locally, against CPU opponents, or online. The controls take a moment to get used to, with each monster feeling a bit sluggish (unsure how intentional this is) but handling well after some time understanding that you're just going to keep pressing block by accident, forever. There's also pickups across the stage to give you or your opponent little boosts, including one displaying the Atari symbol that summons Mothra for an air strike (sure!). The online function seems to work decently well, playing out just as an offline versus match would with only the occasional added jitter (though, admittedly, no connection indicator that I could see).

One thing I do find a tad odd about this demo is its choice of playable roster. Obviously, it's going to be very limited, so there's just three monsters to choose from: Godzilla, King Ghidorah and Gigan. The first two are a given, but Gigan confuses me - while definitely a popular monster, one of the remake's selling points is the addition of the Showa-era Mechagodzilla, which previously didn't appear in the series until the later Godzilla Unleashed. For a demo with a limited cast, why not let people play as the one monster completely new to the game?

All that is to say, I really do recommend this demo whether you played the original or not. And if you like the vibe of kaiju smacking each other around, might I also recommend watching a few of the Showa era films? Start with the original and go in release order, of course. It's fun to watch in real time how Toho takes itself less and less seriously.

Godzilla: Destroy All Monsters Melee Remastered

Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

Rock Paper Shotgun is better when you sign in

#418. New Jersey online casinos win $274.8 million in September, outearning Atlantic City floors

📅 Oct 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

New Jersey's online casinos posted the third-highest monthly win in the state's history in September 2026 and again beat Atlantic City's casino floors. With sportsbook revenue shrinking by double digits, online play is now carrying the state's gaming growth.

New Jersey’s online casinos won $274.8 million in September 2026, up 13.0% from a year earlier, the Division of Gaming Enforcement reported. That beat the $227.8 million won by Atlantic City’s nine casino hotels.

The online figure trailed two earlier months this year. Only July, at $276.9 million, and May, at $276.3 million, came in higher. July rose 12.0% and May rose 11.9%.

The result reversed the order seen a month earlier. In August, online casino win grew only 4.4% to $259.3 million. Atlantic City’s casinos still won more that month, taking $294.9 million despite a 5.5% drop.

The slump on the casino floors came after a stronger summer. Stockton University gaming analyst Brian Tyrrell said the land-based casinos went from a strong May-to-July run to “one of the worst Septembers in recent history.”

Online play moved the other way. The year-on-year comparison is like-for-like on tax. New Jersey raised its gross revenue tax rate on internet gaming from 15% to 19.75% on July 1, 2025. Last September was therefore already taxed at the higher rate.

The state collected $82.7 million in gross revenue taxes from gaming in September. That brought the year-to-date total to $756.8 million. Total gaming revenue for the first nine months of 2026 reached $5.39 billion, 5.1% more than the $5.13 billion recorded over the same stretch of 2025.

Market share is also moving among online operators. BetFanatics’ win jumped 187% to $14.7 million in September.

Sportsbooks had a much weaker month than online casinos. The calendar played a part, with one fewer football weekend this September. Tyrrell suggested that prediction markets such as Kalshi and Polymarket may be pulling bets away from regulated sportsbooks. He added that this could cut state tax revenue.

The sports slowdown is not new. In August, the decline came mainly from a later start to some fall sports, including college football, compared with August 2025. A lower win percentage also weighed on that month.

The split matters beyond New Jersey. That gives lawmakers in states weighing online casino bills concrete figures on tax revenue and on how online play compares with land-based casinos.

Virginia is one of those states. Its House and Senate each passed online casino bills in 2026. The two chambers could not agree on a final version before the session ended on March 14. Virginia will have to try again next year.

Before publication, articles go through a rigorous round of editing for accuracy, clarity, and to ensure adherence to ReadWrite's style guidelines.

#399. Inside the Craft of Peko-Peko branding by Manifiesto

📅 Oct 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Manifiesto's Peko-Peko branding builds a 3D universe where kawaii meets Mexican cariÃ±o, anchored by a balloon-letter logo.

Mexico City studio Manifiesto calls the project "Mexicute is a mood," and the phrase carries the whole concept: Peko-Peko starts from a pair of charming mascots and grows into what the studio describes as a boundless universe, blending the timeless charm of Japanese culture with the vibrancy of Mexican expression. The anchor is the axolotl, Mexico's unmistakable salamander, reimagined as a pink 3D character with a flower crown and a tiny cross-body bag. Around it the studio builds a cast, a cat and a frog, each ready to melt into caps, packaging, and print. The palette does as much work as the characters: bubblegum pink, grape purple, mint, peach, and lime, applied in soft 3D scenes with the kind of squishy lighting that makes every render feel touchable. Nothing here is decorated for decoration's sake; the joy is the system.

The wordmark sets the tone in one gesture: "peko-peko" in fat, lowercase balloon letters, built twice as a 3D object. One version is white and puffy, floating on a purple gradient. The other wears pink glitter with a purple outline and a white cloud halo on peach. The letters read like something you could squeeze. Supporting type stays quiet, a clean grotesque for names and phone numbers on the stationery, so the balloon letters keep all the volume.

Every artifact plays along. The characters become bottle caps: a cat lid on an amber bottle, a frog on a yellow one, the axolotl on purple. A strawberry-scented body wash arrives as a pink bear-shaped bottle wearing the wordmark on its belly. The business card is die-cut with a scalloped edge that quotes the balloon letters. The letterhead keeps the pink wordmark small and centered on white, framed by a single star. It is a world, not a logo sheet.

#273. IEEE Offers More Services to Cities That Want to Be Smarter

📅 Oct 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →



More and more city governments are using advanced technologies to improve livability for their residents. Municipalities use data from smartphones and sensors for traffic enforcement, traffic control, and waste management. They also have implemented artificial intelligence and deep learning programs.

Dubai, Paris, and Coral Gables, Fla., are among the cities that have invested heavily in programs to become smarter.

But along with the conveniences that come with a smart city transition, there have been challenges including safety and privacy issues and eroding relationships between residents and city officials.

Although many initiatives have delivered real innovations, not all have been successful. Inefficient global coordination has hindered projects with unclear standards, socioeconomic differences, and a lack of worldwide governance, leading to a disconnect between the technologies and the ability to adapt to them.

Citizens’ trust in the technologies suffers because of privacy breaches, personal data leaks, and other issues.

Finding solutions

Smart city projects are complex and expensive endeavors that involve politicians, citizens, technocrats, and engineers. Successful projects require citizens to be fully engaged and to have strong collaborations with the participants, especially engineers, as engineering is the cornerstone.

Collaboration with universities, research institutions, and technology experts can bridge knowledge gaps on the behavior of complex socio-technical systems. What’s missing, though, is a global platform where those involved can discuss their experiences.

To help solve those and other challenges, the IEEE Smart Cities Technical Community (formerly the IEEE Smart Cities Initiative) was launched in 2013 by IEEE Future Directions, which incubates emerging technologies and new applications of current ones. The technical community provides city leaders and others with credible, unbiased information and educational content developed by IEEE experts.

Since then, IEEE Smart Cities has launched services including its flagship International Smart Cities Conference, a media center with educational and technical resources, and a contest to recognize the best smart project completed by city officials. The platforms bring together practicing engineers, academic researchers, policymakers, and city leaders to collaborate and exchange ideas and experiences about moving toward a smart-city model.

Better serving humanity

In 2021 the IEEE Power & Energy Society began managing the Smart Cities program, which is supported by the IEEE Communications, Industry Applications, and Intelligent Transportation Systems societies and other IEEE entities.

Thanks to volunteers’ dedication and determination, IEEE Smart Cities launched several services. But the initiative’s structure limited the development of long-term projects and the creation of new activities.

To address the situation, the IEEE Technical Activities Board (TAB) created the second-generation technical community ad hoc committee, also in 2021. It was a pilot program whereby IEEE Smart Cities formed a board of governors to oversee the community’s operations and leadership, and created a policies and procedures manual.

In November 2025 IEEE Smart Cities became a TAB technical community.

IEEE Smart Cities now

Today more than 7,000 volunteers collaborate on various aspects of the community within at least one of the following technical tracks:

  • Smart grid.
  • Smart water management.
  • Green electronics for smart cities.
  • Smart governance and planning.
  • Hydrogen for smart cities.
  • Smart and sustainable campus.
  • Security for smart cities.
  • Smart lighting for cities.
  • Smart mobility for cities.
  • Artificial intelligence for cities.

New webinar series, workshops, conferences, educational resources, and other offerings are being created.

Two events were launched recently: the IEEE European Technology and Engineering Management Summit, held in May 2025 in Belgium, with the next one planned for 2027; and the IEEE Sustainable Smart Lighting World Conference, scheduled for 2 to 6 December in Athens.

The Smart Cities Ambassadors program aims to raise the technical community’s visibility outside IEEE by holding roundtables and other events at non-IEEE functions.

IEEE Smart Cities volunteers are working on launching journals. In addition, standards are being developed in collaboration with the IEEE Standards Association, societies, and councils.

The program is collaborating with the Open and Agile Smart Cities and Communities network, the Instituto de Competitividad e Innovación para el Desarrollo y Transferencia de Tecnología, and the International Project Management Association.

All of those actions have the ultimate objective of boosting IEEE Smart Cities’ growth and sustainability, as well as strengthening the leadership and educational offerings to technical community volunteers.

If you would like to volunteer, visit the Smart Cities website.
