#365. The Public School System Has Failed American Kids. Now What?

📅 Oct 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Writing in the London Times on Sept. 29, author Evan Barker wrote a sobering account about her experience in a Kansas City, Missouri, school two decades ago:

Every day is a fight for survival, not a place to get an education. During passing periods, I’d hurry as fast as I could to avoid being 'jumped.’ I’d walk down the hallways seeing fights break out with alarms blaring and the school’s lone police officer running through masses of pupils after a kid had been beaten up. It’s impossible to learn when you’re constantly in survival mode.

The next day, the Daily Mail published a piece headlined “Huge room full of distraught children confront school district over violent behavior at chaotic New York school.” This was not a troubled New York City school, but a high school in upstate Henrietta, south of Rochester. Students “claim the entire student body has been desensitized to fights, substance use and physical threats.”

This is useful context for Nicole Hannah-Jones’ recent reentry into the national conversation. In her article “In Fighting for Every Black Child, Did I Betray My Own?” she discusses her daughter’s experience in a New York City school. In brief: absent or incompetent teachers; misbehaving and violent students; kids who can’t read or write.

>>> France’s Education Collapse Is a Warning for America

Ms. Hannah-Jones deliberately placed her daughter in New York’s Public School 307, hoping the presence of enlightened parents such as herself would make them better. It didn’t. Neither will more money. Her daughter’s elementary school spends $52,000 per pupil, so each classroom costs taxpayers more than a million bucks a year.

For that, they should expect teachers who show up, grade papers and keep kids under control. They also could hope for better than 17% proficiency in math and 28% in reading at a school with a ratio of only nine pupils per teacher.

Harvard economists Will Dobbie and Roland Fryer found that class size, per-pupil expenditure, teacher certifications and advanced degrees are not correlated with school effectiveness. What is? “Frequent teacher feedback, the use of data to guide instruction, high-dosage tutoring, increased instructional time and high expectations.” The proof of this concept is in the field.

In New York, charter schools—where discipline and accountability are possible—outperform regular city schools in reading and math. These are not bastions of “white privilege,” but highly diverse schools with 95% non-white students. In London, Katherine Birbalsingh’s Michaela School continually churns out top exam results, with a student body that is mostly non-White and non-wealthy.

As a former teacher, my heart sinks for the millions of American kids held captive to left-wing ideology. Their teachers no doubt read Paulo Freire’s “Pedagogy of the Oppressed” in teacher college, because research shows it is one of the most often assigned books in colleges of education. There, they learned not so much how to teach but how to foment activism.

Many of the good teachers move to better schools or simply give up teaching. The worst stay, protected by their labor unions from ever being fired.

>>> Stop Writing Blank Checks to Campuses That Protect Hate

The national teachers’ unions want lifetime employment with no accountability. They spend millions to elect politicians who will pay them more and expect less. They resist efforts to fire teachers for poor work or conduct, implement performance pay, or test teachers’ competence and knowledge. And of course, they most bitterly resist charter schools, school choice, or other attempts to give parents a choice to escape failed schools in the public K-12 system.

Meanwhile, the National Assessment of Educational Progress reveals that students have made no gains since they began tracking performance 30 years ago. iPads, PCs and now artificial intelligence are not magic bullets that can replace study and discipline. Faddish nonsense such as Critical Race Theory, equitable grading, emotional and social learning, ethnic studies, and multicultural awareness takes valuable, limited time away from reading, writing, math and science.

We owe it to our children to end this expensive failure and resume what works. As I wrote in “The Ten Woke Commandments (You Must Not Obey)”, the solutions are obvious, but the teachers’ unions will fight them to the death: no phones in class, student discipline, testing, teacher competency, longer hours, more homework and parental responsibility.

It’s not rocket science. But if we fix K-12, maybe we’ll get some more rocket scientists out of the wasted talent stagnating in schools such as P.S. 307.

#393. SDK Engineer - Python at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

Are fluent in asyncio and have opinions on how a library should serve both sync and async callers.

Know Python packaging for libraries: pyproject.toml, wheels, modern tooling, version support policy, and why libraries shouldn't pin their dependencies tightly.

Test as a habit: unit, type-level and end-to-end against a real stack. Every fix comes with a regression test.

Write well. Design docs, changelogs and issue replies are a large part of the output here, much of it public, and we're async by default.

Have real autonomy: you find the important work, scope it, ship it, and say what you didn't do.

Communicate clearly across both technical and non-technical audiences

Have experience in async or globally distributed teams

Are energized by solving real-world problems for developers

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and moving quickly

#382. Buy The Finished Home, Skip The Remodeling Misery

📅 Oct 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

At almost 50, I’m never doing a gut remodel again.

Everyone who has survived one has the same thousand-yard stare. It’s the look of someone who spent two years arguing about grout color and waiting on inspectors who never show up when they say they will.

Remodeling might be the most unpleasant thing you can voluntarily sign up for. Nobody makes you do it. Yet every year, homeowners line up to hand over hundreds of thousands of dollars for the privilege of being miserable.

Cost overruns are common. So are time overruns.

If you’re brave enough to build a house or do a gut remodel, budget for the project to cost at least 50% more and take 50% longer than expected. If you do, you might come out only slightly bloodied and bruised. If you don’t, and you trust your general contractor’s estimate, your silent stress will go through the roof and detonate every relationship around you.

Here’s a great chart compiled by ResiClub from a report by University of Chicago and Princeton professors titled “Where Is It Slow To Build, And Is It Getting Slower?”

I’m not shocked to see San Francisco at #1, averaging 1.1 years to get a permit and 3.8 years to build a single-family home. San Francisco, after all, is the most expensive city to raise a family.

Miami and Philadelphia are close behind at about 3.4 years, while Indianapolis, El Paso, and Colorado Springs get it done in roughly six months. The national average from permit application to completion is about 8.8 months.

So a San Franciscan could watch a family in Indianapolis build seven homes in the time it takes to finish one. And it’s getting worse. Nationally, single-family permitting time roughly tripled from 1.6 months in 2000 to 4.9 months in 2023.

I’ve gone through two gut remodels and a couple of bathroom remodels since first buying San Francisco property in 2003, and every one has been a hellish process.

My last gut remodel started in mid-2019 and wasn’t 100% done until the end of 2022. Granted, COVID delayed completion by about six months. But three years for a project I thought would take one year at most was brutal.

At least I was able to run out the upstairs portion of the house while the ground floor gut remodel languished.

Here’s what most buyers miss. The construction bill is only the visible part of the iceberg.

Let’s say you buy a $1.6 million fixer and budget $400,000 for a gut remodel over 18 months. Reasonable, right?

Now apply reality. The budget runs 50% over, so it’s $600,000. The timeline stretches to 2.5 years. Meanwhile, you need somewhere to live. At $6,500 a month in rent, that’s $195,000. You’re also paying roughly 1.2% in property tax on a house you can’t live in, about $48,000 over 2.5 years.

Add it up and your $1.6 million fixer actually cost you about $2.44 million. And I haven’t even counted the opportunity cost of tying up roughly $850,000 in cash that could have been compounding in stocks or private investments.

Then there’s the cost you can’t put on a spreadsheet. If your kids are five and seven when you start, they’re eight and ten by the time you move in. You don’t get those years back living out of boxes in a rental while texting your GC at 10 pm.

If you’re over 40 and plan to buy in a city where building takes longer than 1-1.5 years, I highly encourage you to buy a fully remodeled home. You’ll save so much time, money, and headache.

At least in San Francisco, the market undervalues fully remodeled homes. Given how painful remodeling is, they should trade at much bigger premiums than they do. I suspect the same is true in most slow-to-build cities. Take a look at this illustrative chart.

In this hypothetical example, ten years ago, buying a fixer and remodeling it yourself may have saved you roughly $250,000 compared to buying a finished home. That was real money for the pain.

Today, the savings have shrunk to about $60,000. Labor costs, material costs, and permitting delays keep eating away at the discount, while finished homes haven’t gotten much pricier relative to fixers.

Spread $60,000 over 2.5 years of construction and you’re earning about $2,000 a month to manage contractors, chase inspectors, and live in a rental. And that assumes nothing else goes wrong. One bad subcontractor or a three-month permit delay and the savings disappear entirely.

How hard could it be, they ask themselves? Get an architect, hire a reputable GC, pay them to pull permits, pick out the fixtures, and you’re good to go.

But unless you’ve gone through a full remodel or ground-up build, you have no idea how hard things are and how long they take.

Here are some examples of the frustration:

The list goes on. What can go wrong will go wrong, and all you can do is react.

The people working on your house will not operate under the same rules that you do. All it takes is one person or one part to mess the entire remodeling process up.

Do not underestimate how painful remodeling or building is. Your college degree or fancy job title means nothing.

The second reason is more understandable, especially for quirky rich people. Most fully remodeled homes are designed to appeal to the most buyers possible. White oak floors. White quartz countertops. Gray accent walls. Matte black fixtures. Tasteful, safe, and about as exciting as plain oatmeal.

If you want a jade green kitchen, a speakeasy behind a bookshelf, or a Japanese soaking tub with a view of the fog rolling in, no developer is building that for you. You’ll have to do it yourself.

I get it. Your home should reflect who you are. But ask yourself how much you’re willing to pay to express yourself. Paint, lighting, furniture, art, and landscaping can get you 80% of the way to a unique home for a fraction of the cost and time. And the more personalized your remodel, the fewer buyers will want it when it’s time to sell.

Your dream home might be someone else’s gut job.

After all my remodeling experience, I’ll gladly pay a hefty premium for a fully remodeled home and live with someone else’s beige taste. Thankfully, the market hasn’t priced these homes at ridiculous premiums… yet.

Stop being so eclectic and picky. Buy the finished home and then make cosmetic changes if you want.

The noise bullet point is worth a closer look, because it goes both ways.

When you remodel, you’re the villain. Your crew shows up at 8 am with saws and jackhammers, and the retiree next door who just wants a quiet afternoon nap starts plotting against you.

I know because I’m that retiree now. As I wrote in the one negative of living in a nicer neighborhood nobody talks about, five neighbors around me have been doing major remodels in less than a year.

Nicer neighborhoods attract buyers with the money and ambition to rip everything out. So even if you buy a remodeled home, you’re not fully escaping construction. You’re just escaping the bill.

I’ll take that trade any day. Here's a podcast episode I did (Apple, Spotify) talking to my friend Harry on his new home building process.

Some of you will ignore me. That’s fine. It took me two gut remodels to learn this lesson myself. If you’re going to do it, at least do it smart:

The silver lining of any remodel is that when it’s finally done, you’ll feel relieved and maybe even thrilled to have survived the gauntlet.

It’s like hitting yourself in the head with a hammer. It feels amazing when you stop.

Even if your GC and subcontractors do everything right, they still have to get through the plumbing, electrical, and building inspectors, each of whom may inspect twice before the work can be covered up. Once you’re through it, you’ll marvel at all the hard work that went into making your home beautiful. Few things are more satisfying after a long journey.

And if you remodeled thoughtfully, by adding livable square footage or staying within a reasonable budget, you’ll likely get back at least 80% of your money. In cities with wealthier buyers or a booming economy, you may get back over 100% of the cost while enjoying the home in the process.

Just don’t forget to count the years it takes off your life.

Readers, where do you live, and what’s it like remodeling in your city? Have remodeling timelines gotten longer or shorter? How has the cost of remodeling changed? And would you pay a premium for a fully remodeled home to skip the process altogether?

If you still want real estate exposure but never want to deal with permits, inspectors, or a GC who ghosts you, consider investing passively. One area I’m watching closely is the real estate behind AI. Every AI model needs physical data centers, and the biggest tech companies are spending hundreds of billions a year to build them.

Take a look at Fundrise, which manages over $3 billion for more than 350,000 investors and primarily invests in residential and industrial real estate in the Sunbelt, where valuations are lower and yields are higher. You can start with as little as $10, and the platform does all the work while you nap in peace. Fundrise is a long-time sponsor of Financial Samurai.

I’m in the mountains of Colorado. We broke ground on our home in May, and we’re 3 days away from getting our certificate of occupancy. We’re 2 months ahead of schedule and we came in slightly under budget. We heard all the horror stories, how my wife and I would end up broke and divorced in the process, how it would take longer than planned, run over budget, etc.

My biggest advice is to vet and find great subs (not an easy task), and then treat them ridiculously well. We brought a grill to the build site and cooked out burgers, brats, steaks 3-4 days a week. We had coolers filled with ice and their favorite drinks daily. I ate lunch with them a few days a week to bullshit with them, learn about them, their families, etc. Multiple subs on multiple occasions told me they never get treated like this. We also set aside a budget specifically for cash tips to our subs.

Ultimately, though, we had a lot of luck on our side. Every sub is a gamble and we were fortunate that we loved every single one of them.

Simultaneously, we have friends building and they simply hired a GC and never even met their subs, even during the build. They’re over a year behind schedule and are now in a lawsuit against the GC for shoddy work from his subs.

The upside is lower supply and higher prices for existing, owners. So if you never have to, Remodel, you are good to go.

#295. Nekome: Nazi Hunter Director On Whether The Game Is Political, Art Inspiration, Metaphysical Themes, Steam Demo, And More

📅 Oct 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I've been tracking Nekome: Nazi Hunter here at Game Informer since its reveal earlier this year. I finally went hands-on with it during Gamescom 2026 and relished just how disrespectfully brutal it is to Nazis. You will soon get the chance to experience that brutality yourself because developer ProbablyMonsters is releasing a demo on Steam soon on October 19, and it will be available through October 26.

Ahead of that demo launch, ProbablyMonsters also released a new trailer highlighting the game's narrative and villainous Hyena troupe of Nazi leaders as part of today's Galaxies Autumn Showcase. These Hyenas are at the center of protagonist Vano's Nazi hunting, and they are a notorious squad of Nazi agents whose extreme brand of cruelty made them feared across occupied Europe and the world, according to a press release. Players will control Vano as he kills countless Nazis in violent and bloody ways while tracking down each of the Hyenas (who he will presumably also kill violently and bloodily).

Check it out in the latest Nekome: Nazi Hunter trailer for yourself below:

Game Informer also spoke to Nekome: Nazi Hunter game director Jeronimo Barrera about the game in the lead-up to this trailer and the incoming Steam Next Fest demo. In the interview, which took place over email, Barrera talks about the game's politics (or lack thereof), the Hyenas, visual inspiration, and more:

Game Informer's Wesley LeBlanc: How did the team decide on the Hyenas as the antagonists/villains of the game, rather than say, the obvious choice of Hitler?

Barrera: As we developed the narrative, we had already committed to pursuing the grindhouse aesthetic that would drive the presentation. We needed a disgusting set of villains that would be fun to hunt down and murder. Although we are rooted in real-world events, the player fantasy we are after is to hunt down and murder as many Nazis, both big and small, and that needs to deliver over-the-top fun.

Can you tell me about the Hyenas? What are their differences from a narrative perspective, and what are their differences from a gameplay perspective? Does each Hyena approach the hunt for Vano in different ways?

We introduce the Hyenas almost immediately in the narrative. After that, the player won’t be confused as to why they need to hunt them down, not the other way around! Vano hunts them down, and each Hyena boss fight is a unique challenge that pushes the players' abilities.

It sounds like Vano will be hunted across occupied Europe and beyond? What can you tell me about that beyond?

Our research into how we can tell a new story and give a new experience has given us a lot of surprising locations to explore. For example, we were shocked when we realized that we could have our first encounters with Nazis on U.S. soil! Vano’s adopted home is New York City. It’s hard to believe, but there were Nazi Party rallies in New York. This is where Vano starts his hunt through a tragic coincidence, which then takes Vano to Europe. But he’s not being hunted; he is the hunter.

Is there anything larger than life or supernatural or demonic happening narratively, or is it Vano versus some Nazis that are led by the Hyenas? I ask because scenes in this new trailer almost imply some monstrous transformation, or maybe even something vampiric, as blood drips from the Hyenas' faces (though I recognize that could just be a style choice).

The Nazis were hideous monsters. We play on those themes for our abilities and how we present them to the player. There are themes we explore that could be considered metaphysical. But with that said, it’s up for people to interpret the narrative on their own.

How would you describe the art style/visual design of the game?

Our biggest inspiration came from the grindhouse film genre. Turning the stylized pulpiness and violence of movies like Inglourious Basterds into a video game was a challenge that the team was excited to tackle, and we believe this grindhouse spirit gives our game a distinct visual style.

Graphic novels were another major influence. Our goal with the art style was to create something over the top and entertaining, while respecting the serious subject matter. The game would lose its sense of fun if it looked too real, and graphic novels helped us find a look that is visually interesting yet able to handle the story’s darkness and violence.

How long has the team been working on the game?

The team began working on the game in 2024. The genesis of the game was a meeting inside ProbablyMonsters where we started quoting Inglourious Basterds, and we all became set on the idea of making a game about hunting down and killing Nazis through an untold Second World War story.

Nekome: Nazi Hunter’s development is led internally at ProbablyMonsters by Brett Bourbin, Brand Inman, Sergei Kuprejanov, Kay Willis and me, with assistance from co-development partners.

What has it been like working on a smaller title like this and what does your AAA experience bring to the project?

Going from concept to the player’s hands in 18 months is an epic lift that’s only possible with amazing devs that bring extraordinary experience and expertise. The AAA sensibilities run deep at ProbablyMonsters; there are some legends in our house. What makes us different is that our independent approach allows us to tell unique stories and make experiences like Nekome that would have a hard time getting to market.

After Gamescom, ButWhyTho? published a story about how Willis said the game isn’t political, and it caused a lot of discussion/backlash about the game. I wanted to see if the team had any follow-up on the idea that Nekome isn’t political now that some time has passed. At least from my perspective, all art is political; not to mention, making a game where you’re very much visibly and graphically killing and mutilating Nazis is sending a very clear message, especially in 2026 when we have people proudly stating they are a Nazi today. But that’s just my line of thinking – I’m curious where the team lands on this discussion now that some additional time has passed.

I think we're overcomplicating this. Nazis are bad. That shouldn't be a political statement, just common sense. Nekome is a revenge story about people who suffered horrific atrocities at the hands of Nazis. We're not making a statement about modern politics. We're telling a story with villains who are unquestionably evil.

If someone feels politically attacked by a game where you kill Nazis, maybe they should take a long, hard look in the mirror.

Anything I missed you’d like to touch on?

We created Nekome because we saw an opportunity to tell a Second World War story through a perspective that games have rarely depicted. World War II is one of the most common settings in media. And yet, there are almost no games that explore the persecution of the Roma and Sinti during that period.

That is a real, deeply horrible part of history, so our story also comes with responsibility. The team has worked with historians and cultural consultants to help us approach Vano’s identity, his community and the historical context thoughtfully and credibly. We promise to tell Vano’s story honestly and approach that history with care.

Bad signal or dead zones? Download your issues before you leave and read anywhere.

#414. Site Reliability Engineer at Supabase

📅 Oct 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

Supabase manages millions of Postgres instances and is growing. We have strong teams across observability, release engineering, and incident management — and we're concentrating our reliability efforts into a dedicated SRE practice that ties the discipline together across the platform.

You'll be embedded within Service Operations, and your primary job is to make every engineering team more reliable — not by owning their infrastructure, but by establishing the practices, frameworks, and feedback loops that let them own reliability themselves. You'll work across the org: sometimes setting the standard, sometimes pair-programming a fix, sometimes helping a team define their error budget, sometimes telling them it's exhausted.

This role is ideal for someone who has a strong vision for how SRE should work and thrives in async, fast-paced environments where influence matters more than authority.

Partner with service teams to define meaningful SLIs and SLOs grounded in customer experience, and build the error budget policies that turn them into engineering decisions

Own and evolve the Operational Readiness Review (ORR) process — conducting reviews for new services and major changes across observability, alerting, runbooks, capacity, and graceful degradation

Strengthen the incident-to-improvement pipeline: connecting postmortem findings to operational readiness gaps, identifying repeat failure patterns, and driving systemic fixes

Act as the reliability expert teams pull in for architecture reviews, failure mode analysis, dependency mapping, and resilience design

Identify and quantify operational toil across the org, and build or advocate for automation that eliminates it

Help teams design sustainable on-call practices: alert quality, escalation paths, runbook coverage, and noise reduction

Track and report on org-wide operational maturity, surfacing systemic gaps and driving remediation

Have 7+ years of experience in SRE, production engineering, or reliability-focused roles, including experience shaping SRE practices and driving adoption across engineering teams

Have a software engineering mindset — you write code and build tools, not just configure them

Have hands-on experience defining and operationalizing SLOs/SLIs at scale, including error budget policies that actually influenced engineering decisions

Have deep experience with incident response, postmortem facilitation, and turning incident learnings into systemic improvements

Have worked with large-scale multi-tenant systems (bonus: managed database platforms or Postgres)

Are proficient with cloud infrastructure (AWS preferred) and infrastructure-as-code (Pulumi preferred, Terraform/CDK also acceptable)

Communicate clearly and persuasively — this role requires influencing without authority across a distributed org

Have experience in async or globally distributed teams

Are energized by making other teams more effective rather than being the one who fixes everything

Experience with Kubernetes-based platform operations

Familiarity with OpenTelemetry, VictoriaMetrics, Grafana, or similar observability tooling

Experience building developer-facing reliability tooling (SLO dashboards, ORR frameworks, toil tracking, DORA metrics)

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

#301. Castlevania: Belmont&#039;s Curse Review - Worthy Of The Whip

📅 Oct 13, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Castlevania has been a dormant franchise for years, but Belmont's Curse revives the series with fiery passion. There are hundreds of indies inspired by Konami's gothic vampire-hunting games, putting a lot of pressure on the publisher to live up to its far-reaching legacy. Konami's surprising, yet effective solution is to bring in a new developer: Evil Empire, best known for its work on the Castlevania-inspired Dead Cells. The resulting game has a fresh perspective, a brand-new protagonist, and whip-smart execution.

Rose Belmont and her father Trevor have been summoned to Paris, a city under supernatural siege. Rose is eager to prove her worth, partially to impress her father, who killed Dracula 23 years ago, and partially because she wants revenge for her bewitched arm, a curse from Dracula, passed down from her late mother. It's a solid premise, and while the narrative is not particularly complex, Rose's character arc has standout moments that will stick with me.

For newcomers, Belmont's Curse stands alone perfectly fine, though it is technically a sequel to Castlevania III, which the first two seasons of Netflix's Castlevania are based on. Arguably, it's a better sequel to the show than the game; the art style and character designs have clear anime inspirations, and some voice actors reprise their roles. I enjoyed seeing them in a new light, especially as they passed the torch to Rose.

The best example of this torch-passing is a deck of tarot cards, gifted to Rose by her late mother, Sypha. It's my favorite mechanic in Belmont's Curse. Each is a specially crafted vessel to hold the power of Paris' most powerful figures, friend or foe. As you approach each target, Rose will gather clues from her surroundings to aid in hunting them down or besting them in battle. This investigation ranges from just-okay, linear moments in the early game to cryptic, map-spanning missions in the late game. Creatively designed quests to chase down the last handful of tarot cards were my favorite moments in the whole game.

Once you find your targets, you absorb their souls into a tarot card, allowing Rose to speak with them. What starts as a lonely journey becomes almost like a road trip, as Rose has a packed backseat of antagonists and allies who can chime in whenever they see fit.

Each card also unlocks a spell, ranging from fireballs and a magic cross to a blast turning foes to stone. They also come with a list of Works of Mercy: achievements that upgrade your spells upon completion. These range from "Use This Spell 20 Times" to "Kill 44 Skeletons" or "Find All The Rooms In The Clock Tower." It provides a fun reason to use abilities you might not otherwise, which is especially nice when you become overwhelmed by options in the game's closing hours. I was swayed on more than one spell after achievement-hunting for upgrades.

Speaking of combat, the action in Belmont's Curse is magnificent. Rose is light on her feet and only becomes lighter as she gains new abilities, becoming a whirlwind of death by the game's conclusion. Between six weapon categories, a variety of perk-granting relics, and your deck of cards, players are flooded with choice in how to approach encounters. I constantly switched up my loadout, often mid-fight, to adapt to the enemies that appeared. Fights were tough enough to keep me on my toes but never insurmountable, and a forgiving checkpoint system keeps repeated deaths from becoming frustrating. Inventive and intense boss battles against Dracula's coterie of minions are also a major highlight.

After 16 hours, I ended my playthrough with 92 percent completion. I just couldn't resist doubling back to earlier zones to see the new areas I could access with my newfound traversal abilities, and Belmont's Curse never failed to reward me. Between two dozen weapons to find, upgrades to health and mana, and even some huge, optional areas, I'm excited for the satisfaction that awaits me as I dive back in to reach 100 percent.

Belmont's Curse is a strong reminder of why the Castlevania series is so influential, with a rich world, tense combat, and a map chock-full of secrets. While this latest entry certainly pays homage to the Castlevanias of yore, it also breathes fresh life into the franchise, making me excited for its future. Castlevania is back, and between Rose Belmont and Evil Empire, it's in great hands.

Editor's Note 10/13/26 12:18 PM ET: The original version of this review incorrectly stated that Trevor Belmont is voiced by Richard Armitage in Belmont's Curse. We have adjusted the text to remove this reference.

Bad signal or dead zones? Download your issues before you leave and read anywhere.

#272. Edge AI for Smart Cities: From Insight to Cognitive Action

📅 Oct 12, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Not registered yet? Click here to register now.

Already registered? Click here to log in to the hub.

First register your details to create a user profile for the hub, then login to access all content within the hub.

Already registered? Click here to log in to the hub.

Smart city deployments have historically relied on cameras, sensors, and dashboards that capture data without delivering timely insight. Edge AI changes this by letting inference happen close to where data is generated. This supports faster decisions, stronger data sovereignty, and lower operating costs than cloud-only designs. This guide traces the progression from automation and perception to prediction, agentic coordination, and physical AI, where urban systems continuously perceive conditions, reason about them, and act within a tiered edge architecture spanning far-edge sensors, regional infrastructure, and the cloud. It also outlines the durability, security, and interoperability requirements that production-grade edge hardware and software must meet to support public safety, mobility, and infrastructure use cases at city scale.

#425. Cloud Platform Engineer at Supabase

📅 Oct 09, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

As a Cloud Platform Engineer at Supabase, you’ll join Cloud Engineering, part of Engineering Operations, and shape the foundations that let engineering teams build, ship, and operate infrastructure safely and independently across AWS and GCP.

This is a role spanning cloud governance, platform engineering, and hands-on operations. You’ll work across AWS and GCP account structure, identity and access management, Infrastructure as Code (IaC), reusable platform primitives, security boundaries, and self-service workflows for engineers.

You’ll be joining at a point where many of these foundations are being actively defined or reworked. We’re looking for someone who can take ambiguous, messy, business-critical infrastructure problems and turn them into pragmatic systems, tools, standards, and written guidance that scale with the company and empower teams to go faster.

Designing and implementing cloud governance across AWS and GCP, including identity, account organization, access controls, and guardrails.

Building reusable infrastructure and building blocks using Infrastructure as Code (IaC), enabling teams to provision and manage infrastructure safely and consistently.

Creating self-service capabilities that reduce organizational friction and let engineers move more quickly within sensible boundaries.

Owning resource governance and visibility: management tagging, a trustworthy inventory of what exists and who owns it, and consistent cross-account billing data.

Developing reusable security primitives, including secrets storage, KMS, certificate management, token vending, direct service-to-service authentication, mTLS, and related platform services.

Owning cloud-level network and operational primitives: IP address management, DNS, and health signals such as service-limit and quota alerting.

Combining software engineering with hands-on operations and total ownership: building, deploying, monitoring, troubleshooting, and improving what you own.

Establishing and sharing cloud engineering best practices through documentation, internal tooling, examples, and enablement.

Have 5+ years of experience in cloud governance, platform engineering, and/or operations, have operated production systems at scale, and carried on-call for them.

Have previously undertaken a significant migration, e.g. rearchitected an AWS account topology, introduced IaC for existing infrastructure, centralized identity/SSO, or brought a second cloud under governance.

Enjoy building internal platform and self-service experiences for other engineers.

Are genuinely deep in at least one—ideally two—of the three strength areas below (and tell us which is your center of gravity):

Tooling & ecosystem: You’re intimately familiar with the breadth of AWS services in general, and IAM/SSO in particular. You know their Well-Architected Framework, and when to reach outside. You have working knowledge of GCP. You use IaC routinely and have run it in production, whichever tool. You’ve probably collaborated with auditors on meeting compliance requirements.

Software engineering: You write real production code (Go, Rust, or a comparable systems language), and can build reliable, maintainable services. You thrive across all stages of the SDLC, set technical direction, know how to de-risk projects, and produce written artifacts such as RFCs.

Operations: You’ve run production infrastructure yourself: carried the pager, written the alerts and runbooks, used SLOs or similar to decide what to fix first, and made shared systems less risky over time. You like owning systems, simplifying complex infrastructure problems, and turning best practices into reusable building blocks.

Are comfortable treating infrastructure as software and have relevant experience with IaC and automation.

Communicate clearly, work with a wide range of stakeholders, and thrive in async, globally distributed teams.

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and iterating toward better systems over time.

#378. Cycling 3,000Km From Samarkand Through the Pamirs

📅 Oct 07, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

We do a lot of stories about adventurous Westerners, so it’s a treat when we’re reminded how many local people from every corner of the globe feel the same itch to wander.

On August 1, 39-year-old Ravshan Amirov from Samarkand, Uzbekistan, left his city’s famous Registan Square. He cycled east through Tajikistan into China, then turned northwest through the edge of the Tien Shan mountains into Kyrgyzstan. Finally, Amirov finished in his own country, in the town of Andijan, on September 2.

He covered over 3,000km in 33 days, camping much of the time, though he did stay in some small hotels and accepted hospitality when strangers offered it.

Amirov works as the library director at the Samarkand International University of Technology — an atypical adventure background. But three years earlier, at Registan Square, Amirov met a French couple who had cycled through 15 countries in four months. Their journey inspired his own. He then spent roughly a year preparing —training, acquiring equipment, and studying books and films about long-distance cycling. He even ran a marathon.

Yet already on the third day, in Dushanbe, Tajikistan’s capital, he discovered that his preparations had not been ideal. His bike and gear felt like they weighed a ton. He spent a day pruning his gear and making a few repairs, which were already necessary.

Troubles continued. His sleeping pad punctured, forcing him to put in a brutally long day to reach a town large enough to have a hotel where he could repair the puncture. “This is a bitter lesson to learn,” he commented on his Instagram expedition page, VeloLife.

Large parts of his journey followed the Pamir Highway, which had some smooth paved sections but also bone-jarring rough patches. About halfway through, he reached the toughest section of the ride: a three- to five-day stretch where he had to carry all his food and water. “Very scary,” he mused in the lead-up.

A rough and dusty section of the Pamir Highway. Photo: Ravshan Amirov/VeloLife

Indeed, Day 18 proved the hardest. He rode all day and didn’t see a single person or vehicle. “I’ve never seen such roads in my life,” he wrote. “I was preparing for something terrible, but it turned out to be horror squared.”

A lonely road. Photo: Ravshan Amirov/VeloLife

By the 22nd day, things began to pick up. He crossed into China, where the road was “smooth as glass.” He spent an extra day soaking up the sights of the ancient city of Kashgar.

A day off in Kashgar. Photo: Ravshan Amirov/VeloLife

The Ak-Baital Pass was always going to be the crux. At 4,655m, it was the highest part of his route. He reached it on Day 26. Although temperatures in his hometown of Samarkand drop to near zero in winter, he had never slept outside in such cold, and his sleeping bag was inadequate.

“It was the first time in my life I was this cold,” he said. “It was just unreal.” He drank a whole pot of coffee in the morning to warm himself up and improve his morale.

After almost a month, he finally reached the Kyrgyz border. The Tien Shan Mountains affected him deeply. “It was so beautiful that I stopped every 100 meters,” he said. “I was overwhelmed with happiness. In all my 29 years, I don’t think I’ve ever been happier.”

On Day 32, he overnighted in the Kyrgyz city of Osh, then cycled back into his native Uzbekistan the next morning, arriving in Andijan after a short two-hour jaunt. From here, as planned, he caught the train back to Samarkand.

Jerry Kobalenko is the editor of ExplorersWeb. One of Canada’s premier arctic travelers, he is the author of The Horizontal Everest and Arctic Eden, and has just finished a book about adventures in Labrador. In 2018, he was awarded the Polar Medal by the Governor General of Canada and in 2022, he received the Queen’s Platinum Jubilee Medal for services to exploration.

ExplorersWeb is part of AllGear Digital's portfolio of media brands.

Are comfortable with Postgres and familiar with row-level security.

Work async by default. Supabase is fully remote. You make decisions in writing and move work forward without meetings.

Owned auth or identity inside an integrated cloud platform.

Ran an organizations, tenancy, or SSO+SCIM launch from start to finish.

Designed auth for agents or other automated systems: OAuth for MCP clients, machine-to-machine credentials, scoped tokens.

#360. Common Parking Rules Drivers Often Get Wrong

📅 Oct 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Parking rules are as important as driving rules, and although some vary from city to city, most are the same everywhere.

Driving around the country is a favorite American pastime, and I’ve certainly done my share. When I arrive in a city in which I’ve never been before, I like to ditch the car for a while and walk around. But that can be risky if you don’t know the parking rules that apply there. And given the traffic flow modifications that many cities have recently adopted, the rules (some of them, anyway) seem to be changing faster than drivers can keep up.

Where can you park and where can’t you? If parking is legal, how long can you stay without getting towed, and how much will it cost if you get towed? But really, there’s no mystery, according to auto insurance specialist Melanie Musson.  “Knowing where you can and cannot park is pretty easy if you pay attention.”  All you have to do, she says, is follow the signs.

You can always park in a municipal parking lot or parking garage, as long as there’s room and you pay the fee. It’s usually also OK to park on the street, especially if other cars are there, but if the space you choose has a meter, it’s important to feed it. Fines in congested urban areas can be expensive. The cost of a meter violation rose by 40 percent in San Francisco since the pandemic and can set you back more than $100, according to the San Francisco Standard.

Signs usually identify the places you can’t park, as well as curb colors and symbols, such as the universal handicap symbol. Anyone who has taken a driver’s test should know them, because they’re the same all over North America. Engineer Paul Dorr of The Traffic Group refreshes your memory. You can’t park in front of:

Double parking, which means parking alongside a car that is legally parked, is also a violation that some big cities take seriously, even if you do it for only a few minutes. The base fine in New York City is $115, and it rises quickly if you do it repeatedly.

If you see a sign near an open parking space, be sure to read it. It’s probably advising you:

When you pull into a space with a meter, read the meter label before you feed it, because in most cities, parking in a metered space is free after a certain time of day, usually 6 or 8 PM. You might not have to pay.

The label also tells you the rate per hour and, if the meter takes payment cards, it usually provides basic instructions for using one.

In increasingly congested cities, cars compete for curb space with bike lanes, commercial loading zones, pedestrian walkways, outdoor dining, bus stops and EV charging stations. To manage all this, some cities prohibit street parking in certain areas altogether.

Two big changes across the country have an increasing impact on your ability to park. One is the sheer number of cars on the road, which has grown steadily over the years, jumping by 13 million in 2023 alone. The other is that some cities — San Francisco, San Jose, Austin and Hartford among them — have reduced available parking to make room for more buildings. This puts a premium on parking spaces, and meter rates may be significantly higher or vary according to time of day or demand in these cities.

The signs near an available parking space may be advising you of alternate street parking. That’s a parking rule that requires drivers to move their cars from one side of the street to the other on specific days and times so street sweepers can clean along the curb.

“Typically,” explains Dorr, “parking is restricted for one day during the week between certain hours so the street sweeper can clean that side of the street. The following week the parking restriction is on the other side allowing the other side of the street to be cleaned.” Needless to say, you can get a ticket if you ignore these signs, and your car could get towed or sideswiped by a street cleaner.

If your car gets towed, it’s important to retrieve it quickly, because storage fees accumulate every day the car is impounded. You can usually find out where it is by calling the local police non-emergency line. According to Dorr, you ‘ll have to pay some or all of the following fees:

“Look up the municipal government page (such as the local Department of Transportation or Parking Authority) for the city you plan to visit,” advises Dorr. “They often list street sweeping schedules, permit zones, and meter rates.” In addition he suggests consulting a parking app like Spot Angels or ParkMobile, or a GIS parking restriction map on your mobile device for the city you’re visiting.

You can park on any street as long as there are no painted curb colors or posted signs. To avoid a fine, you must stay the legal distance from fire hydrants and intersections, you can’t block a driveway, and your car must be within 18 inches of the curb. Some cities require you to turn your front wheels toward the curb when parking on a slope and fine you for non-compliance.

It’s pretty standard, but it can vary depending on the city. According to Dorr, the standard rule is 15 feet. Two outliers are Washington, D.C., where it’s 10 feet, and Salt Lake City, UT, where it’s only 5 feet.

This depends on local laws. It’s generally legal unless “no overnight parking” signs or signs advising of alternate side parking are posted. In some residential neighborhoods, you may see a sign advising that a permit is required to park there. “If there are “permit parking only” signs and you don’t know what a permit means,” says Musson, “that’s a good sign that you don’t have a permit and can’t park there.” She also advises paying attention to what other drivers do. “If no cars are parked on the street, that’s a good sign that it’s not allowed.”

We recommend our users to update the browser.

#429. Head of AI Native Operations at Supabase

📅 Oct 05, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We are looking for a Head of AI Operations to own how Supabase works.

Supabase is building an AI-native internal operating system: a common way of working across the company where AI carries a meaningful share of the operational load rather than sitting alongside it as an assistant. We are standing up a new central team to build those systems, enable the teams, and embed AI operations throughout the organization. This is the role that runs it.

You will own the maturity model teams are assessed against, the standards that define how work happens here, and the operating cadence, under one design rule: every recurring meeting has to justify why an automated report cannot replace it. You will lead a small team of senior individual contributors, one building the platform and one running enablement.

The premise is that process encoded as agents scales where process enforced by people does not. Your job is to prove that is a repeatable pattern rather than an anomaly, across teams you have no authority over.

We are fully remote and async. The document is the enforcement mechanism here, which means the standards themselves are your deliverable.

Own the maturity model end to end: The rubric, the scoring method, the evidence standard, and the appeals path. Every team ends up with a published assessment and a written plan to its next level.

Run assessments and commitments: Conduct assessments with department heads yourself, landing them as commitments with named owners and dates rather than opinions.

Own the standards layer: Define how work gets tracked, decisions get proposed and resolved, teams migrate onto shared tooling, and stale documentation gets retired. Standards ship as documents teams follow without being chased.

Fix the decision process: Ensure written proposals reach resolution instead of accumulating unresolved by defining stages, exit criteria, and closing mechanisms.

Run the operating cadence: Lead automated weekly reporting, a regular blockers-and-decisions forum, a monthly leadership review, and a quarterly board-level format. Delete meetings a report could replace.

Manage rollouts: Execute private assessments first so leads see only their own and have time to move, followed by a pre-committed public transparency date.

Wire standards into onboarding: Ensure every cohort arrives pre-trained rather than inheriting nearby habits.

Build and direct the team: Hire, grow, review, and direct two senior individual contributors (platform and enablement) without doing their work for them.

Measure and publish outcomes: Measure and publish whether the thesis holds by tracking how much coordination work agents absorb versus what a human program office would cost.

Every standard you write should be the input to something that enforces it, and every enforcement mechanism should produce the evidence that grades it. When you write the rule that work must carry a current status, you are not finished until a report finds the stale items and tells their owners without you in the loop. The same applies to your own function: the team you build gets assessed by the model you own, published next to everyone else's, and you should want that.

The default outcome for a company-wide standard is that teams politely ignore it. So the first question about any standard is not "is this correct" but "what will make people route around this," and the design follows from that answer. Same for meetings: rather than asking what a new sync would accomplish, ask what report would make it unnecessary and build the report. Same for the public transparency date: it gets committed before anyone has a score to defend, because afterwards the lobbying is rational.

You direct agents rather than doing the work by hand, and you can tell whether the direction was any good. That means reading an agent workflow definition and knowing the procedure is too broad, reading a data model and challenging the metric behind it, reading a pull request from your own team and sending it back. Your working artifacts are files in version control that agents can read, not chat transcripts. You will not build the platform. You will be the person who says whether what got built is right.

Scale & Cadence Experience: Have run operating cadence and program discipline at a company crossing ~200 to 800 people while remote and async.

Agentic AI Expertise: Have personally built agentic AI workflows that others depended on. You can read/review an agent workflow definition, a data model, and a pull request to judge quality.

Standard Adoption Track Record: Have taken a standard from zero to company-wide without direct authority over adopters, with a clear mechanism that made it stick.

Async Communication Skills: Are an exceptional writer who leverages documents as enforcement mechanisms.

Accountability: Are comfortable being graded publicly by the system you build alongside every other team.

Have run a maturity model or capability assessment across teams you did not own, published results, and driven organizational reallocations.

Are native to Git, Markdown, and Pull Requests (whether through engineering or technical operations background).

Have permanently eliminated a recurring meeting by replacing it with an automated system.

Deep software engineering background is a plus, but not required.

Standards land without chasing: Rules are written, published, wired into onboarding, and followed because non-compliance is automatically visible.

Universal coverage: Every team has an evidence-backed assessment, owner, and written path to the next level, with disagreements handled through published appeal channels.

Firm transparency dates: Assessments transition from private to public on pre-committed dates without exception.

Net-negative meeting time: By year end, the operating rhythm consumes less calendar time across the organization than when you arrived.

Autonomous operational function: Your two reports operate independently against written charters, at least one non-reference team runs a genuinely automated production workflow, and you focus on review rather than execution.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

#375. Waking Up Inside an Image: A Surrealist Guesthouse, Restaurant, and Bookshop in Mexico City

#82. The part-timers who won three Wembley finals in a row

📅 Sep 30, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The part-timers who won three Wembley finals in a row

Question: Which two men's club sides have won three successive competition finals at the new Wembley?

You probably knew Pep Guardiola's Manchester City won three League Cups on the bounce between 2018 and 2020 before adding a fourth in 2021 â but another side did the silverware hat-trick first.

Whitley Bay, who play in the ninth-tier Northern Football League, won three successive FA Vase finals between 2009 and 2011 â an unprecedented achievement in non-league football.

The FA Vase, a national competition for amateur teams, was established in 1974; and in more than half a century The Seahorses are the only ones to win it four times â including those three on the trot.

And they did so under the eye of an unassuming IT manager from Sunderland, who used "quiet moments in the day" to plan historic cup success.

Ian Chandler, who had three spells at Whitley Bay as a player, became manager in 2004 and spent 10 years in charge.

It was no mean feat, as any non-league manager will tell you. Instead of focusing on work meetings, Chandler says he spent more time sorting hotel bookings or discussing scouting reports.

His team were inspired by missing out on Wembley in 2007-08, when Whitley Bay reached the FA Vase semi-finals. They lost 4-0 to Lowestoft Town in the first leg, and despite going 3-0 up inside 20 minutes of the return fixture at Bay's Hillheads Park stadium they were unable to overturn the deficit.

After that elimination, Chandler developed a superstition of never again giving pre-match interviews - as the last time he did so was before the 4-0 defeat.

Whether because of his renewed focus, or because of a desire among players to make amends, it steeled the group to make history.

"It's the hardest thing to keep a team together - as soon as players get noticed, they get picked up at higher level," Chandler tells BBC Sport.

"But after we got to the Vase semis, players wanted to stay and prove what they could do.

Ian Chandler played for Barnsley, Stockport and Aldershot before a successful non-league career

Despite being a lifelong Sunderland fan, Chandler admits basing his team on Kevin Keegan's Newcastle - with entertainment even placed above winning.

"There was a standing joke in the team, two or three at the back? One time we chased a game with two at the back," he says.

"The Premier League can be so boring; fans want to be entertained, that's what we promised."

Chandler was already a Whitley Bay hero when he was appointed manager by chairman Paul McIlduff, having scored the winner in the 2002 FA Vase final against Tiptree United at Villa Park.

After a slow start as the then-35-year-old with no prior managerial experience found his bearings, Chandler built a talented team which became the envy of non-league football nationwide.

"We played good football. We had pace on the wing, fantastic midfielders, and strikers who were deadly," says McIlduff, who has been Whitley Bay chairman since 2001.

"When we brought them in, it was about fitting the psychology of the dressing room. No-one thought they were better than anyone else, they played for each other."

A growing influence in the side was the 'Whitley Bay Maradona'.

Paul Robinson came through Newcastle United's academy before a career spent across various non-league clubs in the north-east â including Whitley Bay from 2007 to 2013.

Like several other players and staff, he worked with Chandler both in his day job at software company Sage and at Hillheads Park.

"He was my manager at work and football, I was sick of him," he tells BBC Sport. "But it helped get time off for matches."

And it meant he also had an understanding boss if his mind drifted at work.

"Zoning out happened regularly, picturing scoring and winning, what the opposition would be like - hoping they were rubbish so we could have an easy stroll," he says.

Perhaps Robinson's greatest Whitley Bay moment came in the 2010 FA Vase semi-final second leg, when his last-minute diving header in the driving rain secured a 6-5 aggregate victory over Barwell, sparking a pitch invasion.

"Everyone goes to the finals, you were there, you could enjoy it," says Robinson, who is now academy head at South Shields.

"But the rounds before were the bigger games. At home, we would get 2,000 or 3,000 at every game. You felt like a real player. You associate those memories with 90s FA Cup you never think it will happen in non-league."

Chandler played in a 2-0 win over Preston in round two as they reached the FA Cup third round for the first and only time in the 1989-90 season, and they also progressed to round two the following campaign.

Those seasons of success - made The Bay a feared side for non-league clubs across England, and meant they were ready for the big games in a way most amateur sides are not.

"One quarter-final against Biggleswade [in 2009], they turned up in suits and ties," says Robinson. "We thought, this is their day out, their cup final. We won 5-2.

"By the end, teams were turning up afraid of us. We had it won beforehand."

Paul Robinson scored the fifth goal in Whitley Bay's 6-1 win over Wroxham in the 2010 FA Vase final

Off the pitch, the team's success had an influence on the whole of Whitley Bay. A working-class town near Newcastle, which had its heyday as a Victorian-era holiday destination, sporting success brought local pride to the north-east coast.

And like any lower league club, that success could only be supported by dedicated volunteers, who went into overdrive before the Vase finals.

They would go door to door across shops in Tynemouth, North Shields and Whitley Bay to raise support. A van selling tickets was parked outside Woolworths on the high street.

"There was such excitement around the whole of Whitley Bay - every shop had things in the windows, the blue and white stripes," says one of the volunteers, Jenn Robinson.

She got involved because her late husband Mike was a club board member and if she wanted to see him she "had to go around there".

"Everyone wanted to come and watch, we got so many local sponsors," adds Jenn.

"I still do buffets and things for players and long-standing fans. We have a game tonight, and I'm making the sandwiches now."

Newcastle United helped out with selling tickets for the finals, while North Tyneside Council provided security services as thousands flocked to Hillheads Park for big cup games.

But even the best laid plans can go awry.

"We organised a train down to London, the guarantee was that it would go straight to Wembley," says McIlduff. "The first time it worked, second time they were taken to Kings Cross - we had to organise taxis up to Wembley."

By hook or by crook, The Seahorses army made it three times to the national stadium. Some even got royal treatment.

"It was absolutely surreal, and a delight," Jenn says. "The first Wembley final we played at, it had just opened. My husband being on the board, we sat in the royal box, which was fabulous.

"One of the security guys showing me to our seats said, 'Miss Robinson, this is Prince William's seat'. He hadn't even sat in it at that point."

Thousands of fans travelled from Whitley Bay to London across the three finals

Chandler, whose son Benjamin was a mascot at all three finals, said it was an overwhelming experience at times for the players too.

"The first year was crazy, everything was brand new," he says. "The first year the bus driver scraped the side up of the bus driving into the ground.

"The second year we tried to take everything in, all the lads had their mobiles. Then the third year, it was just a case of can we do it, win three in a row."

And three times, Whitley Bay rose to the occasion.

After beating Glossop North End 2-0 in 2009 in front of 12,200 supporters, Whitley Bay returned a year later to thrash Wroxham 6-1.

The opening goal was scored by prolific striker Paul Chow after only 21 seconds. At the time this was the quickest goal in a Wembley final and was only surpassed 13 years later when Ilkay Gundogan scored for Manchester City inside 13 seconds in the FA Cup final.

The hat-trick was completed in 2011 with a frantic 3-2 win over Coalville Town, who equalised twice before Chow was again the hero with an 86th-minute winner.

In total, nearly 30,000 fans were in the stands across the three finals.

After each final, Whitley Bay celebrated in The Green Man pub just 15 minutes' walk from the famous Wembley arch.

"In the first year we just went back to the bar and it was an anti-climax," Robinson says. "The second and third years the club got their act together and we rented a room; families, friends and fans were invited, they were brilliant nights.

"In 2010, we actually spent 10 days together - the week of the final we spent as a squad preparing, then we went to Marbella for five nights. Best 10 days of my life!"

The 2010 trophy lift was perhaps the most emotional though, as the players were joined by former team-mate Mark Taylor. He had played in the previous year's final, before being diagnosed with motor neurone disease, forcing an early retirement.

"Mark used to come on the away trips after he got diagnosed, he just wanted to be Mark - we had a good laugh everywhere we went," says Chandler. "He's still knocking about, abusing me on Facebook. He was the core of our second and third year."

Whitley Bay lift the trophy in 2010, joined by former team-mate Mark Taylor (left)

Success ensured the lives of Chandler and his team were never quite the same again. Reporters turned up to Sage to speak with the manager and his players; even Chandler's mum was interviewed by BBC Radio Newcastle legend Paul Dixon.

Chandler - and his wife - even got up close and personal with managerial icons.

"I was invited to the annual sports awards in Manchester. There were people there like Sven Goran Eriksson and Sir Alex Ferguson [and] numerous others," he says.

"I had my wife with me and Sven came over gave her a peck on the cheek - she wasn't aware of who it was until I told her later that evening."

Robinson admitted that going back to work at an IT company after Wembley glory was a "massive comedown".

"One year I got a standing ovation when I walked into the office, but after that it was just back to normal," he says.

"People wind you up about it - 'are you talking about Wembley again? Give it a rest Robbo!"

Like all good things, Whitley Bay's golden era came to an end. Chandler departed in 2014 after a dip in form; the club have not won a major honour since the 2011 FA Vase and remain in the ninth tier.

But the memories in the football-mad north-east remain.

"Me and my wife went out on a Sunday at a pub in Durham just a few weeks back, and this guy came up out the blue and asked, 'Are you Ian Chandler?'" he laughs.

"Then he started talking about that team; in the north-east, everyone follows football. It was so lovely."

#430. Staff Engineer, Developer Infrastructure at Ava Labs

📅 Sep 29, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Looking to join a world-class blockchain development team? Ava Labs makes it simple to deploy high-performance solutions for Web3, led by innovations on Avalanche. The company was founded by Cornell computer scientists, who partnered with Wall Street veterans and early Web3 leaders to execute a promising vision for redefining the way people build permissionless networks. Ava Labs is redefining the way people create value with Web3. Join us as we empower people to easily and freely digitize all the world’s assets on one open, programmable blockchain platform.

We're hiring a Staff Engineer for the Developer Infrastructure team within Platform Engineering at Ava Labs. Platform Engineering builds and operates the core of the Avalanche network: consensus engines, the P-Chain, virtual machines, and the systems that let many virtual machines interoperate as one network.

Your job is to make the teams building those systems faster and more confident in what they ship. You will work across Platform Engineering to understand where development is slow, fragile, hard to reason about, or insufficiently automated, then build durable infrastructure, tooling, shared knowledge, and technical processes that make better engineering outcomes easier and more repeatable.

The work will typically span CI/CD, ephemeral environments, automated end-to-end testing, chaos and resilience testing, observability, performance infrastructure, and developer workflows, but we are less interested in narrow domain ownership than in the desire and ability to reduce engineering friction and risk. This is a hands-on role: you will be in the code, not adjacent to it.

Partner with teams across Platform Engineering to understand how they build, test, release, observe, and debug, then identify what is worth fixing, and how. Illustrative examples:

Support and evolve our CI/CD pipeline, currently built on Bazel and GitHub Actions, into a fast and trustworthy signal of code quality across unit tests, end-to-end tests, performance tests, and other release-critical checks.

Make ephemeral and long-running test environments easier to provision, inspect, compare, and retire.

Evolve observability infrastructure to collect useful data from diverse environments and compare coherently across runs, versions, and configurations to reveal regressions and highlight improvements.

Improve confidence in the correctness and resilience of consensus, networking, and protocol-adjacent systems through end-to-end testing, fuzzing, chaos engineering, and failure-oriented test design.

Turn recurring team pain points into tools, automation and documentation, and spread the context — through design reviews, examples, and technical writing — so teams extend what you build without routing through you.

Do the unglamorous work visibly so others emulate you: debugging flaky tests, tightening release checks, improving documentation, reviewing tool adoption, and following through until a system is genuinely useful.

You are a self-starter with deep curiosity. You are comfortable diving into someone else’s code to understand how it works, and you treat the code as the source of truth.

You have strong engineering judgment, and an instinct for when code, tests, or systems are not maintainable, observable, or reproducible enough. You can explain the tradeoffs behind your calls.

You build cross-team agreement through working software and demonstrated judgment rather than process mandates. Teams trust you because your work makes their work better.

You care about reliability, and you are motivated by building the infrastructure that helps other engineers ship with confidence.

You find the boundaries between application and infrastructure, product code and developer tooling, more fluid than people pretend.

You listen carefully, yet you are still willing to prototype an idea before it is fully proven.

You can make a high bar feel practical. You push for better engineering outcomes in a way that makes people want to work with you, not around you.

You have operated at Staff scope before, regardless of title: you can point to a workstream you created or transformed, one that other teams came to depend on.

7+ years writing production software, with strong ability in Go and/or Rust.

Experience in large legacy codebases with multiple teams and shared ownership boundaries.

Experience building or significantly improving developer infrastructure, CI/CD, test infrastructure, observability, release tooling, or comparable engineering productivity systems.

Strong testing discipline, the ability to diagnose and pragmatically relieve bottlenecks.

Working fluency with Linux, containers, and infrastructure-as-code.

GitHub Actions or similar CI/CD systems; Bazel or similar build systems.

Distributed systems, blockchain infrastructure, consensus systems, P2P networking, or other high-reliability systems.

Kubernetes or similar orchestration frameworks.

Large-scale observability: tracing, profiling, metrics.

Using data to drive performance optimization.

Chaos engineering, fuzzing, property-based testing, and failure-injection systems.

You want to set technical direction without writing code.

You want to own a fixed infrastructure domain in isolation from the teams using it.

You prefer prescribing process over building tools and feedback loops that make better practices easier to follow.

You consider tests, documentation, migrations, debugging, and adoption work beneath Staff-level engineering.

If you've ever thought about joining an early stage Web3 company - this is it! We're a global, world-class team of experts in computer science, economics, finance, marketing, and law with offices in New York City and Miami. We're highly passionate about Web3 and redefining the way people build and use finance and decentralized applications of all kinds.
